Grounded Intent

10 Grounded Intent

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why is recognizing the right object insufficient to authorize a robot to reach for it?

A spoken request names an outcome, not a motion. Between the word and the grasp lie questions no label settles: which feature of the object to touch, where that feature sits in metric space, how hard to squeeze it, and how long the observation behind the answer stays true. A model can name the right object while misplacing its handle by more than the gripper tolerates, can locate it precisely a moment before a conveyor carries it away, or can report high confidence about the wrong object altogether. Once any of these answers becomes a coordinate it looks exactly like a correct one, and the drive that tracks it cannot tell the difference, so a machine that lets such a proposal command motion turns an error in meaning or timing into force against ceramic, glass, or a person. A learned target is safe to pursue only when it arrives bounded in space, effort, and time, with refusal available whenever the evidence cannot support it. Those bounds keep learned intent on the proposal side of the causal boundary, where a mistake can still be refused rather than delivered as motion that cannot be undone.

Learning Objectives
  • Ground natural-language directives and symbolic task operators into metric affordance frames, continuous motion predicates, and verified geometric goal sets
  • Emit the intent lease from parent belief records with explicit error envelopes, task tolerances, and effort ceilings
  • Calculate intent lease expiry as the earliest of the evidence horizon, the administrative lease, and the stopping deadline
  • Decide when grounding ambiguity or a wrong-object premise requires refusal rather than admission
  • Evaluate terminal reachability within one evidence window, screen approach corridors against the forward occupancy of moving people, and select guarded, renewed stages for a longer approach
  • Design hardware lease expiry that revokes motion authority even when the issuing process hangs or crashes
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

10.1 Grounded Affordances

An operator tells the warehouse mobile manipulator to “pick the red mug” from an active takeaway conveyor. The request names an outcome and leaves the grasp pose, approach velocity, contact force, and execution deadline unspecified. Spatial memory has already done its part by recording a belief about the mug’s handle with an evidence epoch, an initial error of 3 mm, and a growth law. Under that law, the moving belt carries the handle outside the 15 mm grasp tolerance 60 ms after capture. Because that record says only where the handle is and how long the claim holds, it neither specifies what the machine may do with the handle nor authorizes actuator torque. Intent bridges this gap by binding the spoken request to an admissible, time-bounded physical goal that the motion planner can pursue.

↩︎ The belief record a lease consumes (evidence epoch, initial error, growth law) is established in The Belief Record Schema.

The reach does not stay inside the arm. On the running machine a slow intent model sits above a fast chunk policy that proposes base velocity and arm targets together in one whole-body stream (Two proposers and the evidence horizon). A wrong or stale mug target can steer the base as well as the wrist. What the permission path grants in answer to the request is therefore deliberately narrower than motion. It grants an intent lease (section 10.8), a record that names a target and effort ceilings but never a path. For the mug, the lease binds the parent belief record’s digest and evidence epoch to a target pose in the handle’s affordance frame, a goal region of spatial and angular tolerances around that pose, and ceilings on tool speed, acceleration, and contact wrench. Alongside those geometric and dynamic bounds, it records the motion predicates already validated, an expiration deadline on the safety controller’s own clock, and the fallback that controller engages when the deadline passes.

Friction cones, force closure, and symbolic planners matter in this layer only because each supplies a field that the safety controller can check without re-running the upstream model: grasp mechanics fix the target pose, goal region, and wrench ceiling below; task operators (section 10.2) and open-vocabulary grounding (section 10.3) determine which predicates and error envelopes accompany a candidate target; and belief drift (section 10.5) paired with kinematic reachability (section 10.6) bounds the expiration deadline and dynamic limits.

This grounding step is where a learned model’s output first turns into physical coordinates and contact limits that the robot can execute. Open-vocabulary object detectors, language models, and vision-language-action policies let operators specify manipulation tasks in plain words rather than through hard-coded skill libraries. What those neural networks emit, however, is only a statistical hypothesis, such as a bounding box, a semantic token, an uncalibrated point coordinate, or a chunk of normalized actions. An objective function that knows nothing of metric clearance, actuator torque limits, or physical time produced it. On the running machine, the division of responsibility is therefore strict: the learned Brain proposes candidate actions, and the deterministic permission path checks and admits them before any drive receives current.

↩︎ The proposal boundary and the permission path are established in The Machine in Five Levels.

Before the permission path admits a proposed target for planning and execution, the target must satisfy three physical requirements:

  1. Metric physicality: The target cannot remain a symbolic label; it must resolve to an explicit geometric feature carrying a six-degree-of-freedom pose, a gripper aperture, and a bounded contact wrench profile.
  2. Bounded error: The proposal must include a certified spatial error envelope that fits strictly within the task tolerances and mechanical effort limits of both the manipulator and the workpiece.
  3. Temporal expiration: Every target must carry an explicit evidence epoch, bounding its valid lifespan as target objects shift and sensory observations grow stale during compute cycles.

Every request traverses an explicit pipeline to meet these three requirements. Sensory perception resolves symbolic task operators into measurable physical predicates, and calibration transforms image regions into metric poses. The permission path then evaluates effort limits, evidence age, kinematic reachability, and dynamic obstacle clearances before committing the target to the motion planner. A vision-language-action (VLA) model (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023) can propose the mug’s target directly, but its deliberative inference loop is slow. The running machine’s intent model executes at 2 Hz and requires 336 ms (derived in The memory wall) to autoregressively decode 7 target values. These values are a 3D position, a three-value orientation (which the producer converts to the quaternion the wire format carries), and an open-close gripper command. The machine therefore splits proposal across two speeds (Two proposers and the evidence horizon). The intent model names the target, and a smaller chunk policy streams motion toward it between intent inferences.

Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Xi Chen, Krzysztof Choromanski, Tianli Ding, et al. 2023. “RT-2: Vision-Language-Action Models Transfer Web Knowledge to Robotic Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.15818.

That split keeps the joints supplied with setpoints, but it does not keep the target true. If the conveyor moves while one intent inference runs, every chunk aims at where the mug was when the model’s frame was captured. One inference outlasts the handle’s 60 ms evidence window more than five times over. When target evidence expires between model inferences, the system must either switch to a high-rate local visual tracker or arrest motion entirely. Section 10.5 quantifies these expiration dynamics. Safe physical agency demands dated geometric evidence, explicit capability limits, and an autonomous permission path capable of revoking execution authority whenever the neural model lags or stalls.

↩︎ The machine’s multi-rate cadences, from motor current loops through safety screening to deliberative planners, are established in Physical time constants and sampling phase lag.

1 Affordance (from the verb to afford): Gibson (1979) coined the noun for what an environment offers an animal able to act on it, and the sense is relative to the agent. A handle affords a grasp only to a gripper whose aperture and wrench ceiling fit it, so the affordance record carries the gripper’s limits as well as the mug’s geometry.

Gibson, James J. 1979. The Ecological Approach to Visual Perception. Houghton Mifflin.

2 Special Euclidean Group: “Euclidean” because its elements preserve distances, “special” because the rotation part has determinant \(+1\), which excludes mirror reflections. A learned regressor carries no such constraint. An unnormalized quaternion or a matrix with determinant \(-1\) is a valid tensor and an impossible pose, so admission rechecks quaternion normalization (section 10.8) instead of trusting the output head.

Grounding the mug grasp starts with naming which physical feature the gripper will touch, expressing that feature in the robot’s base frame, and bounding the forces of contact. In ecological psychology, Gibson coined the term affordance for what an environment offers an agent capable of acting on it (Gibson 1979).1 Robotics turns that qualitative idea into four measurable quantities for the mug handle. The first is a target grasp pose \(\GraspPose = (\GraspOrientation, \GraspPosition) \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) in the robot base frame (where \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) is the Special Euclidean group of 3D rigid-body positions and \(\SpecialOrthogonalGroup\) rotations).2 The others are a unit approach vector \(\ApproachDirection\), a jaw aperture \(\JawAperture\) in meters, and an allowable contact wrench envelope \(\ContactWrenchEnvelope\) bundling three-axis forces in newtons and three-axis torques in newton-meters. Because each usable site on an object imposes different geometry and force limits, each site defines its own affordance frame \(\AffordanceFrame\) attached to the object’s body coordinates. A single mug offers several distinct affordance frames across its surface. Its thin rim tolerates little normal force, its cylindrical wall requires a wide jaw span, and its handle requires a full six-degree-of-freedom (6-DoF) alignment that threads the fingers through the loop without collision.

Contact mechanics dictates the allowable contact wrench. Under the classical Coulomb friction model, tangential contact forces \(\FingerTangentialForce\) remain stable without slipping only when constrained within a friction cone: \[\|\FingerTangentialForce\| \le \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \FingerNormalForce\] where \(\FingerNormalForce\) represents the normal contact force and \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) is the static coefficient of friction. For an idealized two-finger parallel gripper, Nguyen established that a grasp resists arbitrary in-plane disturbance forces if and only if the line connecting the two contact points lies strictly within the interior of both friction cones (Nguyen 1988).

The general formulation operates in wrench space. For each contact point \(\ContactPointIndex\), a grasp map \(\GraspMap_i\) transforms the local contact force \(\ContactPointForce_i\) into an object wrench composed of the force and its moment \(\GraspContactDisplacement_i \times \ContactPointForce_i\) about the object reference frame. Here \(\GraspContactDisplacement_i\) denotes the displacement from the reference origin to the contact point. Mapping the extremal rays of every friction cone into wrench space yields a set of primitive contact wrenches. The grasp achieves force closure when the origin of wrench space lies strictly within the interior of the convex hull of these primitive wrenches, guaranteeing that admissible contact forces can equilibrate any external disturbance wrench. Because fingers can only push into the surface, the net wrench they apply is a nonnegative scaling of a point inside that convex hull, and enclosing the origin strictly inside the hull ensures that a ray pointing opposite to any external disturbance wrench intersects the hull interior so the fingers can scale up their normal forces to cancel disturbances from every direction. Ferrari and Canny formalized this condition into a scalar quality metric. The metric is the radius of the largest origin-centered ball inscribed within that convex hull, and it represents the minimum disturbance wrench the grasp resists per unit of applied normal force (Ferrari and Canny 1992).

Force closure, however, remains an idealized geometric property that specifies stability without bounding magnitude. A physical grasp requires finite capacity. The normal squeeze force must counter the mug’s gravitational load and dynamic accelerations during transit while remaining strictly below structural limits that would fracture ceramic or spill contents. Grasp mechanics: Friction cones and force closure derives the lower bound on squeeze force from Newton’s second law and develops friction cones and force closure from first principles. These dynamic bounds constitute the effort envelope that the Brain proposes and the permission path enforces.

Turning a spoken request into a checkable physical target is the robotic form of the symbol grounding problem (Harnad 1990), which asks how arbitrary symbols connect to measurable sensorimotor states.3 For the mug, that connection runs through the calibrated transform chain from the camera frame to the manipulator base, the handle’s affordance frame, and the contact wrench bounds. An uncalibrated detection box or an autoregressively sampled coordinate carries none of those bounds. The permission path must therefore catch and reject an unsupported model proposal (a hallucination, in machine learning terms4) deterministically, regardless of the model’s reported confidence. When a spatial feature instead bundles a calibrated base-frame pose, certified wrench limits, and an evidence timestamp, it forms a grounded affordance.

3 Symbol Grounding Problem: Harnad (1990) compared the problem to learning Chinese from a Chinese-to-Chinese dictionary alone, in which every definition points only to more undefined symbols. A model’s text embedding is that dictionary. Only a calibrated camera-to-base chain breaks the circle for the mug, so the proposal must carry its evidence record and not only its label.

Harnad, Stevan. 1990. “The Symbol Grounding Problem.” Physica D: Nonlinear Phenomena 42 (1-3): 335–46.

4 Hallucination: In text generation the word names fluent output that the source does not support (Maynez et al. 2020). For a robot the unsupported output is a coordinate, and nothing in its format marks it as unsupported. Containment therefore has to be a check on evidence that the model does not produce.

Maynez, Joshua, Shashi Narayan, Bernd Bohnet, and Ryan McDonald. 2020. “On Faithfulness and Factuality in Abstractive Summarization.” Proceedings of the 58th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (ACL), 1906–19. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2020.acl-main.173.
Definition 10.1: Grounded affordance

Grounded affordance is an affordance expressed in the robot’s own frame and units: a feature pose in the base frame, an approach direction, an aperture, and a contact wrench ceiling, each tied to dated sensor evidence through a calibrated measurement chain.

  1. Significance: It turns a label into quantities the permission path can check. The mug handle’s grounded affordance is a pose that must land inside a 15 mm task tolerance from evidence with a 3 mm initial error, and on the moving belt that evidence stays valid for only 60 ms (section 10.5).
  2. Distinction: Unlike a detector box or a language label, which designates where an object appears in image space or what it is called, a grounded affordance defines where and how an end effector may establish contact. Unlike Gibson’s perceptual affordance, it is bound to one gripper’s limits and to one capture time.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a learned grasp score or a confident detection as grounding. Neither carries a metric error bound, a wrench limit, or an evidence epoch, so neither can be admitted or expired.

A high-level task action like Grasp(Mug, Handle) specifies an operational objective, but its preconditions require rigorous physical verification. Perceptual noise and latency prevent open-loop execution from guaranteeing that the handle remains unobstructed or that the jaws are properly aligned. Motion predicates formalize these physical prerequisites by mapping abstract conditions to measurable geometric tolerances and wrench readings:

  1. Pre-contact approach predicate: \(\ApproachPredicate(\ToolCenterPointPose) \iff \|\ToolCenterPointPosition - \PregraspPosition\| \le \PositionalTaskTolerance \land \arccos(\ToolCenterPointAxis \cdot \ApproachDirection) \le \OrientationTolerance\), requiring the tool center point (TCP) frame \(\ToolCenterPointFrame\), whose origin sits between the gripper fingertips, to align with the affordance approach axis \(\ApproachDirection\) within spatial tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\) and angular orientation tolerance \(\OrientationTolerance\).
  2. Guarded contact predicate: \(\GuardedContactPredicate(\ContactWrench) \iff \GraspForceFloor \le \FingerNormalForce \le \GraspForceCeiling\), checking a normal squeeze band under the illustrated parallel-jaw model. Here the contact-force magnitude denotes the relevant normal squeeze at each finger, measured or conservatively inferred under qualified contact geometry; a generic force-vector norm or net wrench does not establish that quantity. The task profile sets the band from friction, payload load and acceleration, and crush limits. Passing it supports retention under those premises but does not establish object identity or task completion.
  3. Spatial clearance predicate: \(\SpatialClearancePredicate(\CartesianPosition, \SpatialMemoryMap) \iff \min_{\ObstaclePoint \in \SpatialMemoryMap} \|\CartesianPosition - \ObstaclePoint\| \ge \PointClearanceMargin\), evaluating candidate point \(\CartesianPosition\) against obstacle geometry stored in the spatial memory map \(\SpatialMemoryMap\). This point clearance test serves as a fast preliminary check. Certifying the physical gripper, manipulator links, and their swept transit volumes requires a continuous path collision query during trajectory synthesis.

Together, the spatial and angular tolerances delineate an admissible geometric goal set \(\GoalSet \subset \SpecialEuclideanGroup\): \[\GoalSet = \left\{ \Pose = (\DirectionalCosineMatrix, \CartesianPosition) \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup \;\middle|\; \|\CartesianPosition - \TargetPosition\|_{\ToleranceEllipsoidMatrix} \le 1, \; \angle(\DirectionalCosineMatrix, \TargetOrientation) \le \OrientationTolerance \right\} \tag{1}\] Contact and obstacle clearance predicates operate as distinct, phase-specific execution constraints outside this static pose volume. Within equation 1, the weighted ellipsoidal norm \(\|\CartesianPosition - \TargetPosition\|_{\ToleranceEllipsoidMatrix} = \sqrt{\sum_{i \in \{\Glyph{x},\Glyph{y},\Glyph{z}\}} \left(\frac{\PositionComponent_i - \TargetPositionComponent}{\TaskToleranceSemiAxis_i}\right)^2}\) bounds spatial deviation within an ellipsoid whose principal semi-axes are \(\TaskToleranceSemiAxes = (\XTaskToleranceSemiAxis, \YTaskToleranceSemiAxis, \ZTaskToleranceSemiAxis)\). Here \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\) denotes the corresponding semi-axis \(\TaskToleranceSemiAxis_i\) along any individual coordinate direction. The rotation error metric \(\angle(\DirectionalCosineMatrix, \TargetOrientation) = \arccos\left(\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\DirectionalCosineMatrix^\top \TargetOrientation) - 1}{2}\right)\) bounds geodesic orientation divergence on \(\SpecialOrthogonalGroup\), equivalent to \(2 \arccos(|\UnitQuaternion \cdot \TargetUnitQuaternion|)\) when unit quaternions represent orientations. Terminal grasp poses falling outside \(\GoalSet\) lead directly to task aborts or damage. Translational errors exceeding \(\TaskToleranceSemiAxis_i\) cause gripper fingers to impact the ceramic rim or close on empty space, while angular misalignments exceeding \(\OrientationTolerance\) jam the fingers against the handle opening, tripping joint torque protection or shattering the object.

For the warehouse mug, the nominal task profile assigns all three ellipsoidal semi-axes to the 15 mm grasp tolerance. A terminal TCP offset of 10 mm along the \(\Glyph{x}\)-axis yields a normalized ellipsoidal distance of 0.67, remaining well within the admissible goal set. Conversely, an offset of 20 mm yields a normalized distance of 1.33, falling outside the set and triggering immediate rejection. Checking angular alignment requires the angular tolerance \(\OrientationTolerance\) specified in the verified task profile, which the mug model registers during commissioning.

This goal set is an operational task specification rather than an estimate of target pose. The task tolerance region defines the maximum terminal error that the physical grasp can absorb; state estimator covariance captures uncertainty in the target’s actual pose. For the mug pick, the approved task profile permits at most 15 mm of translational discrepancy. The proposed affordance must therefore supply a conservative target error bound that fits inside this envelope, leaving sufficient headroom for the motion controller’s dynamic tracking error. Artificially expanding the task tolerance to accept an uncertain perception estimate corrupts the safety invariants of the physical task.

Generating candidate affordances prior to intent proposal packaging typically relies on a learned 6-DoF grasp generator applied to an incoming point cloud. Architectures such as Contact-GraspNet predict grasp poses anchored directly to surface points in cluttered point clouds (Sundermeyer et al. 2021). Frameworks like AnyGrasp predict dense \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) grasp distributions with learned quality scores and track them across temporal observation sequences (Fang et al. 2023). The generator outputs a set of candidate grasp configurations paired with scalar confidence scores. However, a learned score merely reflects the network’s predictive confidence relative to its training distribution. It provides neither an analytic stability metric like the Ferrari-Canny radius nor a measured empirical grasp success rate for this specific gripper and workpiece geometry.

Candidate poses surviving initial filtering must satisfy rigorous downstream physical constraints that learned generators omit:

  1. Kinematic feasibility: The manipulator must possess a valid inverse kinematics (IK) solution that avoids joint position and velocity limits. A well-conditioned kinematic Jacobian does not guarantee clearance from mechanical joint stops, making explicit joint travel verification mandatory.
  2. Geometric compatibility: Gripper jaw stroke must exceed workpiece thickness at the grasp site with sufficient clearance margin.
  3. Corridor clearance: The approach trajectory must provide an unobstructed approach vector clear of surrounding bin walls and adjacent cargo.

When no candidate grasp satisfies these constraints, or when target identification remains unresolved across ambiguous visual candidates, the control pipeline must not execute an argmax guess. Instead, the proposal’s producer on the application processor issues an explicit intent grounding refusal (ERR_GROUNDING_AMBIGUITY), signaling a typed rejection of the goal itself. A planning failure means that the planner has not produced a collision-free path to a valid, well-formed goal. An intent grounding refusal halts execution earlier. It rejects an ungrounded or ambiguous goal before invoking the motion planner, which prevents unverified guesses from generating physical trajectories. When a symbolic task planner sits above the grasp generator, its discrete task operators must discharge these same geometric and contact predicates before any step of the plan is admitted.

10.2 Task Operators and Geometric Samplers

A symbolic task planner can emit the discrete action Grasp(Mug, Handle) and mark the mug as held in its internal state even when no collision-free inverse kinematics solution reaches the handle, a bin wall blocks the approach, or the mug has not been separated from adjacent items on the belt. The high-level action Grasp(Mug, Handle) inherits from classical planning traditions, where actions are formalized as operators defined by preconditions, an add list, and a delete list. The formalism traces back to the STRIPS5 framework (Fikes and Nilsson 1971) and was standardized by the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) (McDermott et al. 1998). A discrete world state \(\SymbolicPlanningState\) consists of a set of grounded predicates. An operator \(\PlanningOperator\) is applicable in state \(\SymbolicPlanningState\) if its preconditions hold, \(\text{pre}(\PlanningOperator) \subseteq \SymbolicPlanningState\), producing the successor state \[\SuccessorPlanningState = \left(\SymbolicPlanningState \setminus \text{del}(\PlanningOperator)\right) \cup \text{add}(\PlanningOperator) \tag{2}\] A symbolic planner searches sequences of operators, advancing states by the delete-then-add update of equation 2 until reaching a state satisfying the target goal predicates. For the warehouse pick, an operator pick(mug, handle, conveyor) requires preconditions Reachable(handle), Clear(approach), Singulated(window), and HandEmpty, where a singulated window holds exactly one separated candidate object. Upon execution, it adds Holding(mug) and deletes HandEmpty.

5 STRIPS (Stanford Research Institute Problem Solver): The formalism was built to plan for Shakey, a physical mobile robot at SRI (Nilsson 1984), so symbolic planning began on a machine that moved. Its operators treat an action’s listed effects as certain, an assumption that slipping wheels and a misreading camera cannot grant. The gap that geometric samplers and motion predicates close is therefore as old as the formalism itself.

Nilsson, Nils J. 1984. Shakey the Robot. Technical Note No. 323. Artificial Intelligence Center, SRI International.
Garrett, Caelan Reed, Tomás Lozano-Pérez, and Leslie Pack Kaelbling. 2020. “PDDLStream: Integrating Symbolic Planners and Blackbox Samplers via Optimistic Adaptive Planning.” Proceedings of the International Conference on Automated Planning and Scheduling (ICAPS) 30: 440–48. https://doi.org/10.1609/icaps.v30i1.6739.

Abstract symbols disguise the primary computational challenge: verifying whether a predicate like Reachable(handle) holds requires finding a continuous end-effector pose, a valid inverse kinematics solution, and a collision-free joint trajectory. A symbolic planner cannot evaluate these continuous constraints natively. Integrated task and motion planning (TAMP) resolves this disconnect by coupling discrete symbolic search with geometric samplers, computational routines that sample continuous configuration spaces (proposing candidate grasps, inverse kinematics configurations, and transit paths) to certify symbolic preconditions (Kaelbling and Lozano-Pérez 2011; Garrett et al. 2021). For instance, PDDLStream treats each geometric sampler as a generator stream whose continuous outputs certify symbolic facts. The task planner can then make optimistic symbolic progress, backtracking or branching when a sampler fails to find a valid configuration (Garrett et al. 2020). A sampler failure means that the search has not found a collision-free grasp for the given base pose; unless the sampler certifies infeasibility, it does not prove that none exists. The symbolic planner can spend more search effort, reposition the mobile base, or select an alternate interaction sequence.

Large language models (LLMs) increasingly generate the symbolic layer. Systems such as LLM+P prompt an LLM to translate natural-language task specifications into formal PDDL domain and problem instances, subsequently delegating resolution to a classical symbolic planner (B. Liu et al. 2023). While this decouples linguistic parsing from plan generation, neither the language model nor the symbolic planner verifies geometric feasibility or sensorimotor validity against real-world scene observations. Bridging this gap requires mapping each symbolic precondition to a verifiable motion predicate or geometric certificate evaluated against live physical measurements, as summarized in table 1.

In this execution architecture, the predicted effect Holding(mug) remains a conditional hypothesis until the declared terminal pose, contact, and retention checks pass following finger closure. The guarded contact predicate \(\GuardedContactPredicate\) supplies the normal squeeze check under the task profile; it cannot alone confirm that the intended mug is held. A symbolic task plan operates as an intent proposal in the same manner as a neural VLA target. It asserts the preconditions and effects it intends to achieve, while the deterministic permission path verifies whether calibrated physical evidence supports execution.

Grounding an open-ended directive therefore yields a structured, time-stamped intent proposal. The immediate systems design challenge is converting an identified image region into a calibrated metric target without corrupting its semantic identity, understating its measurement uncertainty, or obscuring its evidence epoch. Once packaged with these invariants, the proposal enters the independent permission path to verify physical admissibility, effort bounds, and expiration limits before execution begins.

10.3 Semantic Request Translation to Geometric Proposals

When an operator asks for the red mug and the vision backbone instead binds the word “mug” to an adjacent table fixture, the coordinate that crosses the proposal boundary arrives in the trajectory pipeline indistinguishable from a valid handle target. Low-level motor drives monitor encoder counts, winding temperatures, and phase currents, so they have no way to tell whether the setpoint they track belongs to a mug or a bolted bracket. Any unmodeled arm compliance or thermal expansion also adds metric bias that a high semantic confidence score never reveals. Before any motion planner accepts a target, the grounding pipeline must therefore turn the model’s selection into a geometric proposal that carries bounded spatial uncertainty, dated selection evidence, and an explicit expiration time.

10.3.1 The four-stage open-vocabulary grounding pipeline

Passing a detector’s 2D bounding box or an uncalibrated pixel coordinate straight to a motion planner hides two failures at once. Two identical mugs can both score high without triggering an ambiguity warning, and a small depth error along the camera ray can stretch into centimeters of pose error in the robot base frame. To expose each failure where it can be checked, the four-stage open-vocabulary grounding pipeline separates semantic referent selection from metric localization and physical actuation:

  1. Stage 1: Visual encoding: RGB-D frames from static or eye-in-hand cameras enter a vision backbone running at batch size one on the machine’s accelerator, transforming raw image patches into dense feature embeddings.
  2. Stage 2: Referent selection: Given a prompt such as “pick the red ceramic mug,” an open-vocabulary detector such as Grounding DINO or OWL-ViT proposes candidate bounding boxes and scores each against the text prompt (S. Liu et al. 2023; Minderer et al. 2022). The model evaluates each candidate independently without global normalization across boxes (OWL-ViT trains with a sigmoid focal loss (Minderer et al. 2022), while Grounding DINO filters tokens against an absolute threshold). Two identical mugs can therefore both yield high confidence, or a lone glass jar can score above threshold even when no mug is present. A promptable segmentation model such as SAM 2 can refine the chosen box into an object mask and track it across video frames (Ravi et al. 2024). However, a 2D bounding box or segmentation mask provides only image-space localization without metric depth, surface normals, or wrench limits. It indicates where the object projects onto the camera sensor, leaving contact dynamics and grasp mechanics completely unspecified.
  3. Stage 3: Metric lifting: Calibrated camera intrinsics, depth measurements, and the hand-eye extrinsic transformation lift the selected pixels into 3D points in the robot base frame, while propagating sensor noise into a base-frame covariance matrix. For stereo and structured-light systems, depth error grows quadratically with range, \(\DepthStandardDeviation \approx \CameraFrameDepth^2\DisparityStandardDeviation/(\StereoBaseline \FocalLength)\) for baseline \(\StereoBaseline\) (the stereo term of equation 8.6), focal length \(\FocalLength\), and disparity error \(\DisparityStandardDeviation\), whereas lateral error grows only linearly, \(\PixelStandardDeviation \CameraFrameDepth/\FocalLength\). Their ratio, approximately \((\CameraFrameDepth/\StereoBaseline)(\DisparityStandardDeviation/\PixelStandardDeviation)\), scales with range and baseline rather than remaining constant, while time-of-flight sensors have their own range-dependent error profiles. The resulting uncertainty ellipsoid elongates dramatically along the optical line of sight. Any unmodeled extrinsic error from wrist compliance or mounting deflection tilts this elongated ellipsoid directly into the robot’s clearance margin.
  4. Stage 4: Affordance parameterization: The lifted 3D geometry transforms into candidate actions. Depending on the architecture, these candidates may take the form of scored 6-DoF grasp sets from a learned grasp generator, 3D attraction and repulsion cost fields (Huang et al. 2023), parameterized motion primitives (Liang et al. 2023), or decoded action tokens (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023). Stage 4 filters these candidates against the kinematic reachability, gripper aperture, and collision clearance criteria detailed in section 10.1. It then constructs a goal tolerance set \(\GoalSet\) around each surviving affordance frame and assigns the required motion predicates. Finally, it serializes these hypotheses into a fixed-size, statically verified proposal structure (table 2) and carries them across the physical silicon boundary between the non-real-time Linux Application Processor (AP) and the hard real-time Safety MCU.

↪ The pinhole camera model and the propagation of pixel and depth noise into a base-frame covariance are developed in Camera Projective Geometry and Covariance Propagation.

↪ Byte layout: The intent payload’s fields, types, and offsets are given in Intent payload.

To prevent a learned output from masquerading as a deterministic setpoint, the producer must link the proposed referent to the dated sensor evidence that justified its selection. The upstream observation contract often carries bounding boxes, attention weights, class probabilities, and a full oriented covariance matrix, which are too bulky and variable to send across a real-time link. Instead, the intent proposal references its parent belief record by a SHA-256 digest (which traces data lineage rather than authenticating the sender) and an evidence epoch. Because the digest, a monotonically increasing sequence number, and a trailing CRC-32 checksum belong to the common wire header (Proposal header and chunk payload), table 2 lists only the intent payload. The payload holds the candidate pose, conservative base-frame error bounds, task tolerances, and a requested administrative lease \(\IntentAdministrativeLease\) that caps how long the host may hold the target before the MCU presumes a stall. That duration is only a request, because the MCU clamps it to the evidence horizon and the stopping deadline derived in section 10.5 and section 10.8. Since an object on the belt can shift, roll, or become occluded moments after camera exposure, the permission path immediately rejects any target coordinate that arrives without an active evidence link and a bounded deadline.

When a vision-language model evaluates multiple candidate objects, retaining only the highest-scoring bounding box conceals an unresolved selection. In purely digital applications, a misranked recommendation misleads a user but moves no mass. In robotics, an admitted spatial proposal immediately commands physical momentum. For instance, if a red glass jar sits beside the requested red mug on a conveyor, an uncalibrated tie-break may inadvertently target the jar. This case, the jar beside the mug, returns later in the chapter. The architecture must preserve candidate identity and calibrated uncertainty, rejecting proposals whenever ambiguity exceeds task-specific safety thresholds. The host system can then solicit operator clarification or acquire additional sensor viewpoints, while any previously admitted motion executes under strict lease and fallback guarantees.

Formally, open-vocabulary grounding ambiguity occurs when perceptual evidence supports multiple candidate referents, making the selection of any single target an arbitrary tie-break. Because open-vocabulary detectors score candidate bounding boxes independently rather than generating a normalized categorical distribution across objects, they do not natively report this condition. The host application must therefore construct a distribution explicitly. By collecting all candidate boxes exceeding an initial detection threshold and normalizing their raw scores into a vector \(\CandidateScoreVector = (\NormalizedCandidateScore_1, \dots, \NormalizedCandidateScore_n)\) where \(\sum_i \NormalizedCandidateScore_i = 1\), the system can evaluate candidate spread. Although the Shannon entropy \(\ShannonEntropy(\CandidateScoreVector) = -\sum_i \NormalizedCandidateScore_i \log \NormalizedCandidateScore_i\) quantifies distributional dispersion, an arbitrary entropy threshold provides no rigorous statistical error bound.

Conformal prediction removes this limitation by replacing the single best answer with a set of candidate answers whose coverage holds in finite samples (Vovk et al. 2005; Angelopoulos and Bates 2023). A threshold calibrated on held-out labeled cases keeps every candidate it cannot exclude, so the set contains the true answer at a chosen rate across cases without assuming any model of how the scores are distributed. For example, the KnowNo framework applies conformal prediction to identify when a robot should halt and request human clarification (Ren et al. 2023). On a calibration dataset of \(\CalibrationExampleCount\) labeled scenes, the nonconformity score for scene \(\CalibrationSceneIndex\) is \(\NonconformityScore_j = 1 - \TrueReferentScore\), representing one minus the normalized score assigned to the ground-truth referent \(\ExampleLabel_j\). This construction assumes that the true label belongs to the defined candidate option space in calibration and deployment. An absent or filtered-out true object violates that premise unless the option space explicitly includes a no-valid-referent option calibrated in the same way. The critical threshold \(\ConformalScoreQuantile\) corresponds to the \(\lceil (\CalibrationExampleCount+1)(1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate)\rceil / \CalibrationExampleCount\) empirical quantile of these nonconformity scores. At runtime, the prediction set \(\ConformalPredictionSet\) retains every candidate whose normalized score cannot be excluded at significance level \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\): \[\ConformalPredictionSet = \{\, \CandidateIndex : \NormalizedCandidateScore_i \ge 1 - \ConformalScoreQuantile \,\} \tag{3}\] Provided that calibration and deployment scenes satisfy exchangeability,6 the set \(\ConformalPredictionSet\) is mathematically guaranteed to cover the true referent with probability at least \(1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate\). Under exchangeability, the nonconformity score of a new test scene is equally likely to occupy any of the \(\CalibrationExampleCount+1\) ranks relative to the calibration samples. It therefore exceeds the \(\lceil (\CalibrationExampleCount+1)(1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate)\rceil\)-th smallest calibration score with probability at most \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\). This is marginal true-label coverage across scenes, not the same error bound conditional on singleton acceptance or a guarantee of task success. If the resulting prediction set is a singleton (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 1\)), the pipeline passes its candidate to downstream metric lifting and the remaining admission checks. If \(|\ConformalPredictionSet| > 1\), the producer refuses with ERR_GROUNDING_AMBIGUITY, prompting the system to request clarification from an operator or maneuver for an unoccluded viewpoint before transmitting any motion proposal across the boundary. An empty set (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 0\)) means that no retained option meets the threshold, and the producer refuses it as well. It does not reliably detect an absent mug, because a lone retained wrong jar normalizes to one and survives this score test.

6 Exchangeability: A sequence of random variables is exchangeable when its joint probability distribution remains invariant under arbitrary permutations of sample order. Exchangeability is strictly weaker than assuming independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations, yet it suffices for the \(1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate\) marginal coverage guarantee. Modifying station lighting or altering the conveyor layout introduces distribution shift that invalidates exchangeability, causing coverage guarantees to lapse silently. Calibration records must therefore be maintained on a per-station basis with mandatory expiration horizons, matching the lifecycle of target sensor evidence.

When both the mug and the glass jar appear simultaneously within the pick window, an open-vocabulary detector assigns raw box scores of 0.62 and 0.48 respectively. Normalizing these values yields normalized candidate scores of 0.56 and 0.44, producing a Shannon entropy of 0.685 nats relative to the two-candidate maximum of 0.693 nats. The entropy shows high dispersion across the two candidates. It does not measure the probability that either is the correct object. Conformal calibration instead supplies marginal true-label coverage under the declared option-space and exchangeability premises.

Across a calibration set of 200 labeled scenes with an allowable error rate \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate =\) 0.10, the rank formula selects which calibration score becomes the threshold \(\ConformalScoreQuantile\), the nonconformity score at rank 181 in ascending order. Its value depends on the measured scenes. Assuming that score is \(\ConformalScoreQuantile =\) 0.60, any candidate must achieve a normalized score of at least 0.40 to remain in the prediction set. Because both objects exceed this threshold, the resulting prediction set retains two elements (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 2\)). The producer correctly flags an ambiguity error and refuses to emit a trajectory. Conversely, if the jar scores only 0.10, its normalized value drops to 0.14, falling below the admission threshold. In that contrast case, the prediction set collapses to a singleton (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 1\)), permitting the mug proposal to proceed.

Because conformal coverage guarantees hold marginally across scenes and degrade under distribution shift, production workcells enforce defense-in-depth through deterministic physical invariants. The station therefore adds its own singulation check, a candidate count on fresh depth evidence that runs apart from the grounding model and must find a unique candidate within the pick envelope (section 10.7). This operational gate prevents arbitrary tie-breaking at the station. When multiple objects populate the workspace, downstream motion planners proceed only when perceptual evidence unambiguously discriminates the requested target.

The pipeline therefore spans two processors that share no memory (figure 1). Everything that can be wrong about meaning runs on the application processor, and everything that must hold regardless of meaning runs on the Safety MCU. Its 1 kHz permission loop arms a lease timer that drops authority without any message from the host. That separation buys independence only when memory access, bus timing, and fault containment are qualified in each of three domains:

  • Application Processor (AP Domain): A general-purpose, non-real-time operating system executing on host CPUs and hardware accelerators runs the vision-language backbones and, in architectures that use them, 3D cost fields. If the conformal prediction set over candidate referents contains multiple entries (equation 3), an upstream filter halts execution with an ambiguity alert. When the prediction set yields a unique referent (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 1\)), the AP serializes the candidate goal pose, requested wrench limits, and parent belief digest into a fixed-size, statically structured intent proposal.
  • Board-Level Transport Boundary: The proposal packet crosses from the AP to the discrete safety MCU over an isolated board-level interconnect, such as a Serial Peripheral Interface (SPI) bus backed by Direct Memory Access (DMA). The MCU receiver copies the transmission into a dedicated staging mailbox within local SRAM. The communication channel is treated as untrusted. Embedded CRC-32 checksums, monotonically increasing sequence numbers, and strict hardware timeout limits detect frame corruption, packet loss, and transmission stalls. The protocol design must also account for worst-case bus latency, as the bus interface alone guarantees no real-time delivery bounds.
  • Safety Microcontroller Unit (MCU Domain): Operating on a dedicated real-time core, a deterministic control loop evaluates the incoming proposal as an unverified request. The MCU checks the evidence timestamp against its internal monotonic clock, verifies workspace envelopes and joint reachability limits, and clamps requested forces and velocities to certified plant limits. Furthermore, the MCU verifies that a deceleration trajectory can execute safely within existing clearance margins. The MCU arms a hardware countdown timer to the lease expiry \(\IntentLeaseExpiry\) only after the proposal passes all admission gates. The expiry is the earliest of evidence decay, host silence, and the stopping deadline (section 10.8 formalizes it). The MCU then grants bounded tracking authority to the joint drives.

10.3.2 What each proposal family leaves to admission

This three-domain boundary stays fixed even when the upstream grounding model is swapped for a different proposal family. What changes from one model family to the next is which physical checks remain for admission to supply. An upstream model may emit a 2D bounding box, a 3D cost field, a scored 6-DoF grasp, or a chunk of normalized action tokens. In every case, treating that output directly as a motion command skips the physical checks that prevent an unseen obstacle collision, a joint-limit hit, or an unbraked contact impact. The pipeline’s stages draw on multimodal feature extraction, cross-attention alignment, and open-vocabulary phrase grounding from computer vision and natural language processing (Kamath et al. 2021; Gu et al. 2022; Radford et al. 2021; S. Liu et al. 2023), yet an embodied machine cannot rely on a model’s internal activations for physical safety. For example, a semantic skill ranker like SayCan outputs a discrete skill identifier scored by language affordance and value functions (Ahn et al. 2022). A programmatic generator like Code as Policies produces a control script that calls perception and motion APIs (Liang et al. 2023). VoxPoser synthesizes 3D spatial value maps (figure 2). OK-Robot emits a distribution of sampled grasp poses (figure 3). Because none of these outputs natively carries a bounded wrench envelope, a certified clearance margin, or a hardware lease expiration, choosing one model family over another only shifts where conversion happens and leaves the admission checks in table 3 unchanged.

Kamath, Aishwarya, Mannat Singh, Yann LeCun, Gabriel Synnaeve, Ishan Misra, and Nicolas Carion. 2021. “Mdetr-Modulated Detection for End-to-End Multi-Modal Understanding.” Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision, 1780–90.
Gu, Xiuye, Tsung-Yi Lin, Weicheng Kuo, and Yin Cui. 2022. “Open-Vocabulary Object Detection via Vision and Language Knowledge Distillation.” International Conference on Learning Representations.
Radford, Alec, Jong Wook Kim, Chris Hallacy, Aditya Ramesh, Gabriel Goh, Sandhini Agarwal, Girish Sastry, et al. 2021. “Learning Transferable Visual Models from Natural Language Supervision.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 8748–63.
Liang, Jacky, Wenlong Huang, Fei Xia, et al. 2023. “Code as Policies: Language Model Programs for Embodied Control.” 2023 IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 9493–500.

Predicting a syntactically valid coordinate or control script never guarantees physical feasibility. Vision-language and action models are trained to maximize sequence likelihood over offline demonstration datasets. Their loss functions encode neither metric distances, rigid-body collision avoidance, manipulator Jacobian conditioning, nor frictional contact dynamics. Validating a candidate action requires deterministic physical checks evaluated within explicit coordinate frames. These checks include signed-distance queries over robot and environment meshes to verify clearance, numerical inverse-kinematics solvers with joint-limit and singularity screening, and Coulomb friction models to confirm that the planned grasp wrench resists expected gravitational and inertial loads. For contact-rich tasks such as sliding an object, turning a latch, or executing a handover, the system can simulate candidate trajectories in a rigid-body physics engine before committing actuator current. However, simulation fidelity remains bounded by the accuracy of estimated surface friction and inertial parameters. Downstream controllers must still enforce real-time admission checks regardless of simulation outcomes.

In VoxPoser’s value-map interface (figure 2), an affordance map rewards voxels near the handle the instruction names, an avoidance map penalizes voxels near observed obstacles, and the planner follows the composed cost around the obstacle it can see. Because the planned path is optimal only with respect to what the maps contain, an obstacle that the observations missed adds no cost and the path runs straight through it, leaving clearance against unobserved space to the permission path.

OK-Robot shows the same division in a modular pipeline (figure 3) (Liu et al. 2024). Open-vocabulary detectors (OWL-ViT and SAM) isolate the queried object, a class-agnostic generator (AnyGrasp) samples dense 6-DoF candidates over the point cloud, and geometric heuristics prune them to a single pose. That pose is still an uncertified Stage 4 proposal. Before it can command joint motion, it must be bound to dated sensor evidence, given a wrench envelope, and admitted under an expiring lease on the Safety MCU. Every proposal family in table 3 leaves a comparable residue, which the table’s last column itemizes as the evidence admission must still supply.

Liu, Peiqi, Yaswanth Orru, Jay Vakil, Chris Paxton, Nur Muhammad Mahi Shafiullah, and Lerrel Pinto. 2024. “OK-Robot: What Really Matters in Integrating Open-Knowledge Models for Robotics.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.12202.

Regardless of the specific output architecture, raw actions generated by VLA models carry no certified metric bounds. These heads normalize action spaces relative to empirical training distributions rather than physical workspace obstacles. For example, RT-1 and OpenVLA discretize each continuous action dimension into 256 uniform bins across bounded ranges (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Kim et al. 2024). FAST tokenizes the discrete cosine transform coefficients of a multi-step trajectory chunk (Pertsch et al. 2025). In contrast, \(\pi_0\) generates continuous action chunks through a flow-matching diffusion expert, predicting 50 steps per inference and streaming them at up to 50 Hz (Black et al. 2024). Although each approach outputs kinematically coherent trajectories within its training distribution, none verifies dynamic feasibility or collision clearance against surrounding obstacles. Similarly, spatial location tokens in vision-language models like PaliGemma quantize normalized pixel coordinates on the camera sensor. These image tokens transform into metric poses only after incorporating measured depth maps, camera intrinsics, and hand-eye calibration matrices. The geometric uncertainty propagated through this calibration chain, particularly along the camera’s optical axis, often dwarfs the discretization error of the underlying token grid.

Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Joseph Dabis, Chelsea Finn, Keerthana Gopalakrishnan, et al. 2023. “RT-1: Robotics Transformer for Real-World Control at Scale.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.025.

Table 3 sorts proposals by the representation they send downstream, a classification that outlasts any one model. The systems that fill each role change within a few years, so table 4 dates them separately, pairing what each current system emits with what the permission path must still establish on its own.

Every row leaves admission the same two questions, whether the metric estimate is tight enough for the task and whether it belongs to the right object. The mug’s handle shows how each can fail while the other holds.

10.3.3 Imprecise estimates and wrong objects

When the wrist camera views the red mug from across the workstation before the arm begins its reach, depth uncertainty along the optical ray stretches the 95 percent error region to more than twice the handle’s grasp tolerance. Closing the gripper on that single-frame estimate can therefore drive the fingers into the ceramic rim. Under an illustrative calibrated Gaussian sensor model for that pre-approach wrist-camera frame, the principal standard deviations in the camera frame are 4 mm and 5 mm laterally, and 12 mm along the optical depth axis. Because the optical axis aligns with the gripper’s approach vector, range uncertainty directly challenges position control. A 95 percent statistical region surrounding the estimated handle coordinate \(\CartesianPosition_0\) is defined by the Mahalanobis squared distance \((\CartesianPosition-\CartesianPosition_0)^\top\PerceptionDepthCovariance^{-1}(\CartesianPosition-\CartesianPosition_0)\le\PositionConfidenceEllipsoidRadius^2\). With the three-degree-of-freedom chi-square quantile \(\PositionConfidenceEllipsoidRadius^2 =\) 7.815, the distribution expands into principal semi-axes of 11.2 mm, 14.0 mm, and 33.5 mm.

Against the mug’s 15 mm physical grasp tolerance (figure 4, panel a), the ellipsoid fails on one axis only. While both lateral semi-axes fall comfortably within the mechanical tolerance window, the depth semi-axis of 33.5 mm substantially exceeds the allowed threshold. Consequently, the admission gate rejects the proposal, because the controller cannot guarantee that closing the gripper fingers at that estimated setpoint will capture the handle rather than collide with the rim.

The remedy, worked through in section 10.6, decomposes the reach into distinct phases. The system first admits an intermediate, collision-checked inspection waypoint, allowing the arm to travel closer to the workspace. Once positioned nearby, a second wrist-camera observation constructs a refined belief record, reducing the initial position uncertainty to 3 mm. Because a Gaussian probability region retains an unbounded asymptotic tail, statistical distributions alone cannot certify absolute spatial clearance. The downstream intent proposal therefore needs independently justified conservative error envelopes in the robot base frame before requesting trajectory tracking. Covariance alone supplies only a statistical region.

While a configured statistical gate cleanly rejects detections with large variance envelopes, it cannot catch an entirely incorrect object identified with high semantic confidence. This is a different case from the jar beside the mug, where both candidates entered the prediction set and the producer refused. Consider instead the jar in place of the mug, where a fragile, thin-walled glass jar sits on the workstation where the ceramic mug was expected and no mug is present. When prompted to pick the mug, the vision-language model misidentifies the jar with strong semantic certainty, generating a sharp, low-variance geometric proposal centered on the glass container. The downstream trajectory planner accepts the coordinate, solves inverse kinematics in \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\), verifies joint position and velocity limits, and confirms that the approach path clears all static obstacles in the occupancy map. Because the requested motion satisfies all kinematic and wrench envelopes, the real-time permission path authorizes execution. However, if the gripper applies the clamping force required to hold a heavy ceramic mug, the jaws crush the thin glass container upon contact despite clearing every geometric check.

A confident wrong object passes every geometric check until the gripper closes.

This scenario marks the boundary between physical verification and semantic premises. The deterministic permission path enforces coordinate envelopes, kinematic limits, actuator torque thresholds, and obstacle clearance against known geometric occupancy grids. These physical invariants cannot confirm whether a given coordinate actually corresponds to the requested object. The premise that the targeted item is indeed the requested ceramic mug remains an unverified upstream assumption outside the hardware controller’s reach. Establishing that premise requires distinct task-level perceptual evidence, which must be tracked through the handover release case (Adjudicating the Safety Case) and recorded in the residual-claims register (A residual-claims register). A learned task filter upstream cannot close this gap either. The AutoRT deployment showed that a language-model task filter improved the fraction of sensible task proposals while unsafe commands still passed it. Human teleoperators therefore had to intervene and halt execution (war story 10.1) (Ahn et al. 2024).

War Story 10.1: AutoRT task filtering and supervision (2024)
Context: AutoRT deployed foundation models to propose and filter physical manipulation tasks for fleets of mobile manipulators operating in unstructured human office environments (Ahn et al. 2024).

Mechanism: In a sample of 259 proposed tasks, human annotators judged 228 (88 percent) acceptable. A second LLM pass then filtered the tasks against a robot “constitution” of safety and embodiment rules. Of the 214 tasks it kept, 200 (93 percent) were acceptable. The filter rejected 17 of the 31 unsuitable tasks, a recall of 55 percent, and passed the other 14.

Impact: All 14 misses arose during teleoperated collection. Although the filter raised overall proposal quality, human supervisors had to intervene to intercept every unsafe proposal before robot execution.

Response: The system architecture placed physical safety outside the model’s domain. Deployments supplemented learned proposals with hardware joint-force limits, physical emergency stop buttons, and direct human line-of-sight supervision or physical perimeter barricades.

Systems lesson: A learned task filter can improve proposal quality, but it cannot serve as a safety referee. An independent, deterministic permission and fallback path must validate every physical action against real-time plant limits before execution.

Ahn, Michael, Anthony Brohan, Noah Brown, Yevgen Chebotar, Omar Cortes, Byron David, Chelsea Finn, et al. 2024. “AutoRT: Embodied Foundation Models for Large Scale Orchestrating of Robotic Agents.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.12963.

The distant view and the glass jar mark two distinct verification boundaries. The distant view fails on metric uncertainty along the depth axis, whereas the jar in place of the mug fails on an unverified semantic premise, because metric precision does not establish object identity. A safe geometric proposal must therefore carry both dated physical evidence and task-specific selection evidence across the silicon boundary. By this point the mug proposal holds most of what the lease will carry. The grounded affordance has fixed the target pose and its goal region, and the squeeze bounds of the grasp analysis have set the wrench ceiling. The operator table has named the predicates the grasp must discharge, and the handle’s error envelope has been compared with the task tolerance. Carrying these values is not yet holding authority, however. The proposal still lacks a grant, a deadline, and a stop that works without the proposer, and turning it into bounded authority over the arm is the work of the handoff.

10.4 The Intent Handoff and Bounded Authority

If a deliberative host could stream raw joint trajectories or actuator currents directly to the drives, a two-millimeter depth error into a rigid latch plate or a stalled inference thread mid-reach would drive motor currents to saturation before any host process could intervene. When the arm reaches for the red mug, the deliberative host instead submits a candidate intent specifying a bounded target region, an expiration deadline, and explicit physical effort ceilings. The region and the ceilings are the grounding results packaged for transit, the affordance pose with its goal set and the grasp’s wrench and speed limits, and the requested deadline is computed from the parent belief record’s evidence epoch. An independent real-time supervisor intercepts this proposal, vetting each requested parameter against retained sensor evidence and configured plant dynamics. Granting a target lease merely authorizes downstream trajectory planning within certified safety bounds. The deliberative model remains isolated from low-level actuator registers.

This permission path validates every requested target constraint before any motion planner can generate trajectory setpoints. Rather than forcing memory-constrained microcontrollers to maintain full scene representations, host-side spatial engines export compact geometric evidence across a strictly typed interface. The supervisor verifies four mandatory invariants against this evidence:

  1. Spatial containment invariant: The proposed geometric goal set \(\GoalSet \subset \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) must lie strictly within the robot’s reachable workspace manifold \(\ReachableWorkspace\). The bounded spatial uncertainty envelope \((\XPositionErrorEnvelope, \YPositionErrorEnvelope, \ZPositionErrorEnvelope)\) must fit inside the task tolerance \((\XTaskToleranceSemiAxis, \YTaskToleranceSemiAxis, \ZTaskToleranceSemiAxis)\) without intersecting persistent obstacle geometry in spatial memory \(\SpatialMemoryMap\).
  2. Temporal freshness invariant: The sensor evidence epoch \(\MonotonicClockInstant_0\) must match a retained parent belief record. The unobserved elapsed time on the Safety MCU’s monotonic hardware clock must not exceed the evidence horizon \(\EvidenceHorizon\) that section 10.5 takes from the parent belief record.
  3. Dynamic effort invariant: Requested tool-center-point velocity \(\SpeedBound\), acceleration \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\), and contact wrench \(\ContactWrenchCeiling = [\ContactForceCeiling, \ContactTorqueCeiling]^\top\) must remain strictly within actuator thermal ratings, link inertia limits, and workpiece structural yield thresholds.
  4. Certified deterministic fallback invariant: The robot must possess an independently verified, pre-computed stopping or hold trajectory that brings all moving links to rest within current physical obstacle clearance (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)) if authority is abruptly revoked.

The first three invariants turn the requirements that opened the chapter into checks: metric physicality and bounded error become spatial containment and the effort ceiling, while temporal expiration becomes freshness. The fourth adds the obligation those requirements left implicit, a stop that works without the proposer. Each invariant guards against a distinct failure mode in physical interaction. Loose target bounds can exceed kinematic grasp tolerances, and stale sensor evidence risks collision with a displaced object. Excessive commanded effort can shatter a workpiece, and an unvetted handoff leaves moving mass without a safe recovery trajectory. The resulting intent lease records the validated envelope that survives these four checks and constrains the downstream trajectory planner, which must synthesize path timing and deceleration profiles that satisfy supervisory admission criteria (section 10.8). Pairing the evidence epoch with a finite lease expiration keeps the temporal bound deterministic even if the host crashes, because the safety controller compares its own clock against the admitted deadline rather than waiting for a cancellation packet (section 10.5).

Spatial and temporal bounds alone cannot guarantee safety if the downstream controller commands unbounded effort. Every intent proposal must therefore declare explicit effort ceilings covering tool-center-point velocity, acceleration, and contact wrench. Consider a depth perception error of two millimeters that places a target goal slightly inside a rigid workpiece. An unrestricted position controller will drive actuator currents to saturation trying to eliminate tracking error, imposing severe mechanical stress on the gear train. During a guarded approach to a spring-latched cage door, for example, the host proposes a 15 N tripwire ceiling, well below the 100 N structural latch threshold, paired with an approach velocity capped at 0.03 m/s. The permission path evaluates these values against hardware torque-speed curves and environmental thresholds, admitting only parameters that guarantee structural integrity.

Downstream of the permission path, an impedance controller shapes interaction dynamics, answering a position error with the restoring force of the virtual spring-damper established in Transmission compliance, contact impedance, and tooth fracture. Under quasi-static contact, scaling the virtual stiffness along the approach vector directly limits steady-state contact force for a given positional error. Transient impacts, however, demand more than static stiffness modulation; containing peak collision forces requires coordinating approach velocity, passive mechanical compliance, sensor latency, and actuator closed-loop bandwidth.

Omitting spatial, temporal, or dynamic boundaries leaves physical hardware vulnerable during communication dropouts or perception stalls. Stopping distance scales linearly with the reaction delay preceding brake engagement and quadratically with operating velocity (equation 2.2). For a mobile base navigating within physical obstacle clearances, a silent or stalled proposer inflates this reaction window by the entire lifespan of the active command lease. As established in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget, this chunk lease duration directly extends the unbraked latency parameter \(\PrebrakeDelay\). The verified safety envelope must therefore maintain obstacle clearance greater than the total stopping distance, factoring in sensor pipeline latency, transport jitter, and state estimation uncertainty. Furthermore, powered deceleration requires electrical bus energy and closed-loop control authority to survive an upstream fault. Logic-level power alone cannot generate mechanical braking torque.

The intent lease defines the temporal horizon over which a deliberative goal remains valid. It operates alongside the chunk lease of Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget rather than replacing it, because the two hold different kinds of authority even though the Safety MCU’s permission path checks both. The chunk lease authorizes motion. It allows an admitted chunk to drive the actuators only for its short lease unless the chunk policy renews it at its own rate. By contrast, the intent lease authorizes a goal, fixing which target that motion may pursue, how hard it may press, and until when, and only newer evidence can renew it. Consequently, each lease ends authority from its own side. When the chunk lease lapses, motion stops even though the goal remains valid. When the intent lease lapses, nominal tracking toward the goal ends at once, even if the current chunk still has lease time left, and the MCU engages the validated fallback (The trajectory record and its deadlines). A rejected proposal is the gentler case, because it revokes nothing and leaves already admitted motion running until its own leases lapse. The physical stopping budget must accommodate the cumulative delay across perception, communication, actuator response, and mechanical braking in every case.

A 200 ms unbraked stall drives the latch to 2,400 N, far past its limit.

Napkin Math 10.1: What the latch ceilings buy
Problem: The intent lease for the cage door requests a 15 N tripwire and a 0.03 m/s approach speed against a 100 N latch limit. With the local contact loop in place, what force does the latch see when the brake command issues, and what would it see in a counterfactual design with no local contact trip, where pressing stops only when the proposer or the intent lease acts?

Variables:

  • Tripwire 15 N and approach speed \(\LatchApproachSpeed =\) 0.03 m/s, the requested ceilings; latch limit 100 N (Illustrative)
  • Plate stiffness \(\LatchPlateStiffness =\) 400 N/mm (Illustrative) and contact-loop response 2 ms
  • Unbraked stall \(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant =\) 200 ms (Illustrative), shorter than one 500 ms intent period

Math:

  1. Force at brake command: The tripwire fires at 15 N, and the plate keeps compressing while the 2 ms contact loop responds, so the force when the brake command issues is \(\BrakeCommandForce = \ContactTripwireForce + \LatchPlateStiffness\,\LatchApproachSpeed\,\ContactLoopResponseTime =\) 15 N \(+\) 400 N/mm \(\times\) 0.03 m/s \(\times\) 2 ms \(=\) 39 N. The arm stop that follows has only a fraction of a millimeter of compression left to spend; Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers derives that stop (equation 12.11).
  2. Stall travel (counterfactual, no local trip): \(\FiniteChange \ApproachPosition = \LatchApproachSpeed\,\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant =\) 0.03 m/s \(\times\) 200 ms \(=\) 6 mm past first contact.
  3. Stall force: \(\LatchStallForce = \LatchPlateStiffness\,\FiniteChange \ApproachPosition =\) 400 N/mm \(\times\) 6 mm \(=\) 2,400 N.

Result: The contact loop brakes at 39 N. Without the local trip, a stop that waited on the proposer would let the arm press on, and the spring model predicts 2,400 N.

Systems insight: The predicted force lies far past the 100 N limit, beyond the point where the spring model applies at all; the latch has failed. Sizing and arresting transient contact within milliseconds is the duty of the high-rate, low-latency contact loop. The intent lease supplies the operational boundaries (velocity caps and force tripwires) that keep dynamic loads within the contact loop’s braking envelope, ensuring physical safety even when upstream deliberative proposers stall across multiple intent cycles.

Both the mug grasp and the latch engagement depend on a privilege separation boundary.7 While deliberative models propose goal targets and operational bounds, an independent supervisor enforces safety invariants and executes certified fallback maneuvers whenever evidence expires or contact states change unexpectedly. This design decouples deliberative reasoning from real-time physical protection, inverting the classical priority hierarchy found in early reactive control systems (Brooks 1986).

7 Subsumption Architecture: In the classic subsumption architecture of Brooks (1986), a higher layer subsumes a lower one by suppressing its inputs or inhibiting its outputs. Physical AI systems invert that priority. The lower, real-time layer retains absolute veto authority over deliberative proposals because its timing, stability, and failure behavior can be strictly bounded.

Brooks, Rodney. 1986. “A Robust Layered Control System for a Mobile Robot.” IEEE Journal on Robotics and Automation 2 (1): 14–23.

Structured intent handoffs explicitly disentangle task tolerance, sensor uncertainty, actuator effort limits, and fallback trajectories. External dynamic processes, however, dictate how long these static bounds remain valid. On a moving conveyor, for instance, a correctly localized workpiece drifts continuously after perception capture. The mathematical model governing this uncertainty growth establishes the strict temporal deadline that the independent safety controller must enforce.

10.5 Expiring Goals and Temporal Validity Leases

Suppose the host application processor hangs while the robotic arm reaches for the red mug on the moving belt. The fault that makes canceling the reach necessary is the very fault that prevents a cancellation command from being sent. An explicit abort requires a working sender, scheduler, and network link, while the belt carries the mug beyond the grasp tolerance regardless of when the model last published a coordinate. An intent lease must therefore expire autonomously from the physics of environmental drift. Autonomous expiry applies the third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), to physical goals, so that the permission side and not the proposer decides when authority ends. In distributed systems, Gray and Cheriton (1989) introduced leases to maintain cache consistency: a coordinator grants time-bounded authority that lapses unless explicitly renewed, preventing a crashed client from blocking others indefinitely. That correctness proof assumes bounded clock drift between client and coordinator. In an embodied system, the Safety MCU enforces this assumption by transforming every incoming timestamped epoch into its own local monotonic timebase with a strictly bounded synchronization error. The physical domain raises the stakes, because an expired cache lease merely delays a read, whereas an unexpired motion lease lets moving mass keep consuming physical clearance unchecked. The privileged Safety MCU acts as coordinator, issuing a transient tracking lease to the unprivileged deliberative host. If the host stalls during inference, pauses for garbage collection, or experiences an operating system kernel panic, the Safety MCU’s local hardware countdown expires and automatically engages a validated fallback response. This requires no network packet or cancellation command from the failed host.8

8 Watchdog Timer: An independently clocked down-counter that forces a hardware reset or a safe fallback output unless software reloads it before reaching zero. Because a crashed host cannot reload it, the lease countdown runs on the isolated Safety MCU rather than in the host process it supervises.

9 Monotonic Clock: A counter that only advances forward at a fixed nominal rate, such as POSIX CLOCK_MONOTONIC or a free-running microcontroller timer. A lease evaluated against wall time could gain or lose its entire remaining window in a single clock adjustment. Every measurement in this chapter is therefore evaluated on the Safety MCU’s monotonic counter and converted from external clocks with a bounded synchronization error.

Lease duration is measured exclusively against the Safety MCU’s monotonic hardware clock.9 Network time-synchronization daemons or operator adjustments can step wall-clock time abruptly backward or forward, while host-level software watchdogs can freeze alongside the user-space process they are designated to monitor. Furthermore, no inference pipeline, regardless of its mean throughput, completely eliminates long-tail p99 scheduling stalls. Placing the lease countdown on an isolated, hardware-timed MCU counter ensures that target authority is revoked locally the instant the lease expires. This hardware isolation decouples expiry detection from host operating system jitter, although total physical stopping distance must still account for fieldbus transmission, drive response, and kinematic deceleration.

The deadline the lease enforces is the evidence horizon \(\EvidenceHorizon\) of Two proposers and the evidence horizon put to a new use. There it bounded how long the evidence behind a chunk keeps the tracked mug within tolerance (equation 3.5). Here the intent lease starts from the same quantity, and memory supplies its value. The mug-handle belief record carries an invalidation horizon \(\BeliefInvalidationHorizon\) (equation 9.14), the elapsed time before its error growth law (equation 9.12) reaches the task tolerance. The lease reads that horizon with the grasp tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\) as the error ceiling and the initial sensor error \(\InitialTrackingError\) as the starting bound.

For a conveyor pick, the growth law charges two bounded effects against that ceiling: \[\PositionErrorBound(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) = \InitialTrackingError + \DriftVelocityBound\,\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant + \tfrac{1}{2}\,\SlipAccelerationBound\,\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant^2 \tag{4}\] Here \(\DriftVelocityBound\) bounds the relative velocity between the gripper frame and the target, including both conveyor motion and unmodeled mobile-base odometry drift, and \(\SlipAccelerationBound\) bounds the residual slip acceleration of the mug on the belt. The evidence-only horizon is the elapsed time at which \(\PositionErrorBound(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant)\) reaches \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\). The numerical examples in this chapter evaluate this upper ceiling with zero additional controller tracking reserve. An admission gate, however, must first subtract a separately qualified nonnegative scalar tracking reserve from \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\). It then solves equation 4 against the reduced budget. It refuses immediately if the initial target error plus that reserve already reaches the tolerance (alongside the full 3D error-set containment check of section 10.8). The two limiting regimes are horizons derived earlier in the volume. With no tracker, the horizon is the linear horizon of equation 9.15, with belt speed as its growth rate. With a tracker that cancels belt velocity, the horizon is the slip-only root of equation 3.5.
