In this chapter’s symbols they read \[\UntrackedEvidenceHorizon = \frac{\PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError}{\DriftVelocityBound} \quad (\SlipAccelerationBound = 0), \qquad \TrackedEvidenceHorizon = \sqrt{\frac{2(\PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError)}{\SlipAccelerationBound}} \quad (\DriftVelocityBound = 0) \tag{5}\] These evidence-only roots are upper ceilings on target lifetime. When drift velocity and slip acceleration act together, \(\EvidenceHorizon\) is the positive root of \(\PositionErrorBound(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) = \PositionalTaskTolerance\) in equation 4, which is strictly shorter than either single-effect root.

If \(\PositionalTaskTolerance \le \InitialTrackingError\), the target is inadmissible at the moment of perception. Initial measurement uncertainty alone consumes the entire task tolerance, which obligates the safety controller to reject dependent motion commands immediately. Even when the evidence horizon is strictly positive, granting motion permission requires a separate geometric verification. The system must verify that current physical clearance accommodates total sensing latency, permission evaluation time, fieldbus transmission delay, mechanical brake engagement, and physical braking distance.

For a red mug carried on a takeaway conveyor, the untracked linear root dictates the evidence deadline. Sizing the control pipeline requires determining whether deliberative intent models can refresh targets rapidly enough to renew authority before this window elapses. While the evidence-horizon calculation in Two proposers and the evidence horizon sized the low-level chunk execution policy against this horizon, deliberative intent models evaluate on a substantially slower cycle.

Napkin Math 10.2: The cadence gap
Problem: The conveyor carries the mug at 0.20 m/s. Can the 2 Hz intent model keep the target inside its tolerance by re-proposing it, and how slowly would the belt have to run for a model-only renewal to suffice?

Variables:

  • Grasp tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance =\) 15 mm and initial handle error \(\InitialTrackingError =\) 3 mm (Illustrative)
  • Belt speed \(\DriftVelocityBound =\) 200 mm/s (Illustrative)
  • Intent period \(\IntentProposalPeriod =\) 500 ms (Chosen, 2 Hz) and inference \(\ProposerInferenceTime =\) 336 ms (Derived, The memory wall)

Math:

  1. Horizon (equation 5): with no tracker, \(\EvidenceHorizon = \UntrackedEvidenceHorizon =\) (15 mm \(-\) 3 mm) \(/\) 200 mm/s \(=\) 60 ms.
  2. Error at one period: 3 mm \(+\) 200 mm/s \(\times\) 500 ms \(=\) 103 mm, 88 mm past the tolerance. Inference alone overruns the horizon by 336 ms \(-\) 60 ms \(=\) 276 ms.
  3. Renewal ceiling: Each model-only renewal installs a target computed from a frame captured one inference earlier, and that target stays in force until the next renewal replaces it one period later. Just before the replacement, the evidence behind it is \(\IntentProposalPeriod + \ProposerInferenceTime =\) 836 ms old (the reaction latency of Action chunking, here for the intent model), so the drift it can keep up with is (15 mm \(-\) 3 mm) \(/\) 836 ms \(=\) 14.4 mm/s.

Result: The model alone could renew the target only on a belt running at 7.2 percent of its speed. Even a slow settling drift of 20 mm/s, such as a mug still sliding to rest after the belt stops, whose 600 ms horizon is charted against inference latency in figure 6, is too fast for it.

Systems insight: Physical drift and allowable tolerance dictate \(\EvidenceHorizon\), independent of computing performance. An intent renewal can extend operational authority only if the sum of its dispatch period and inference latency fits entirely within this window. Artificially inflating the lease duration to cover inference delay leaves the pose estimate outside its declared tolerance envelope for 276 ms. Without a qualified, high-frequency local tracker, the system must either slow or stop the conveyor, or refuse the pick task entirely.

The 60 ms limit is a physical evidence deadline that only fresh sensor observations, validated against current motion, can extend. Faster deliberative inference does not lengthen this physical evidence horizon, although a sufficiently fast capture-to-admission pipeline and renewal cadence can fit inside it.

Deploying a qualified local tracker changes these dynamics. By re-measuring the mug in every camera frame and matching conveyor velocity, the local tracking loop prevents conveyor speed from driving error growth. While the initial perception uncertainty of 3 mm still counts against the grasp tolerance, it leaves a target-only error margin of 12 mm for unmodeled physical perturbations before reserving controller tracking error. Sizing against an illustrative worst-case residual slip acceleration of 0.50 m/s², this remaining margin yields the same 219 ms tracked horizon that Two proposers and the evidence horizon computed for the chunk policy. Because the local tracking pipeline runs at high frame rates, only the tracker’s brief period plus sensing latency must fit within this expanded horizon. The tracker also changes who renews the lease. Each tracked frame yields a newer belief record of the same handle, and the producer re-proposes the same referent against it. The Safety MCU therefore receives renewals with a newer capture epoch on the tracker’s cadence rather than the model’s. Relieved of geometric tracking, the deliberative intent model only re-confirms semantic identity (which mug to grasp) at its own rate, and no renewal waits for a fresh inference. The lease attaches its target to the handle’s affordance frame, whose base-frame pose under a qualified tracker follows the belt. The goal set therefore moves with the mug. The tolerance bounds only how far the handle departs from the tracked estimate under residual slip, rather than how far it departs from a fixed point in the base frame. Whichever horizon governs the active operating mode caps the duration of the intent lease granted for that target (section 10.8).

Examining a host processor stall illustrates the protection each horizon provides. Suppose the robotic arm reaches toward the red mug under a target admitted without local tracking, and the application processor stalls during neural network inference. If the system held that unvalidated target across a 200 ms stall, the actual mug position would drift 43 mm away from the commanded setpoint, causing the gripper to close on empty air or strike the rim. The Safety MCU does not rely on host health to catch this fault. In the ideal zero-additional-reserve case, at 60 ms the lease expires automatically, commanding braking to begin within 22 ms (accounting for one permission evaluation tick, one fieldbus cycle, and actuator brake onset), even if the current chunk’s own lease has time left. In contrast, under a qualified local tracker, an identical 200 ms stall leaves the target estimate displaced by only 13 mm, inside the target-only tolerance budget, because the ideal tracked horizon extends to 219 ms. Delegating fast geometric updates to local tracking is what allows a 2 Hz deliberative intent model to operate safely in a dynamic physical environment.

On one clock, the proposer’s timing loses to both horizons (figure 5). Both idealized zero-additional-reserve countdowns end before the 336 ms inference returns and well before the next proposal at 500 ms, so the model can renew neither lease. Absent other renewals, each expiry is followed by the 22 ms stop onset. Under a qualified tracker the tracked countdown restarts with every tracked frame, long before it reaches 219 ms, so the tracked bar in the figure shows what happens only when those renewals stop, as they do when the host stalls. What follows that expiry is still motion, as panel (b) of figure 4 sketches in speed against time. The drive begins braking 22 ms after expiry, and the stopping distance under the falling curve must already fit the clearance that remains. This is why the lease carries the stopping deadline as a term of its own (section 10.8).

Panel (a) of figure 6 generalizes the cadence gap across drift speeds. The 336 ms inference alone fits under the 600 ms horizon of slow settling drift but far exceeds the 60 ms conveyor horizon, and adding the proposal period, as the cadence-gap notebook showed, breaks even the settling case. Panel (b) previews the cheap early filters (sequence and evidence, workspace, transit lower bound, and ambiguity) that reject impossible proposals before the planner spends compute on them. Sustaining continuous tracking safely requires fresh physical evidence, not merely retransmitting a new packet computed from stale sensor observations. Every renewal must derive from an independent physical capture and reach the Safety MCU before the active lease expires. The end-to-end timing budget must account for image capture, inference processing, fieldbus transmission, clock synchronization uncertainty, and permission verification. The timing condition \(\IntentProposalPeriod + \Latency < \EvidenceHorizon\) serves only as a necessary scheduling check. When a renewed proposal arrives, the trajectory planner computes a jerk-limited splice originating from the measured physical state. The safety controller admits this spliced trajectory only if the new swept volume, refreshed evidence validity, and emergency stopping reserves remain fully verified. An abrupt target displacement that violates kinematic or clearance bounds triggers immediate refusal or a controlled stop.

Safely executing a bounded lease presupposes that the commanded geometric target is physically achievable by the robot. If a proposed coordinate falls outside kinematic reach or demands joint velocities that exceed actuator limits across the lease window, granting the lease squanders control authority. It also risks tripping drive overcurrent protection as motor controllers saturate attempting to reach unattainable velocities. Determining whether an incoming goal is kinematically and dynamically admissible must precede trajectory generation, establishing a physical admissibility filter between high-level deliberative intent and real-time execution.

10.6 Admissible Goal Sets

If the red mug rides past on the far side of the belt, beyond the arm’s physical reach from where the base is parked, no sequence of joint torques can bring the gripper to its handle. In an embodied ML system, this physical impossibility must be intercepted at the proposal boundary, before the target enters the trajectory optimization queue. When an upstream vision-language model or diffusion policy emits a candidate Cartesian coordinate, treating that output as an unverified proposal protects the real-time motion pipeline from executing physically impossible trajectories and from wasting compute cycles attempting to solve them.

A reachability screen also protects the planner’s computational budget. When a target cannot be reached at all, the trajectory optimizer faces an empty feasible set. Local nonlinear solvers cannot certify infeasibility quickly. They typically exhaust their entire iteration budget before timing out, transforming an upstream geometric hallucination into compute starvation on the planning core. Similarly, a requested terminal move that cannot complete before its supporting sensory evidence expires must fail before entering detailed planning. Long-horizon tasks should instead request guarded progress under renewable evidence leases rather than attempting an ungrounded terminal grasp on a single sensor capture.

To avoid spending the planning budget on an already impossible request, the admission layer evaluates proposals against two low-cost preliminary checks:

  1. Static Workspace Manifold Check (\(\TargetPose \in \ReachableWorkspace\)): Geometric workspace analysis defines the closed spatial manifold \(\ReachableWorkspace \subset \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) of reachable end-effector configurations given link dimensions, joint travel limits, and mounting geometry (Craig 2005; Lynch and Park 2017).10 An analytical reachability check evaluates whether the target \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) pose \(\TargetPose\) resides inside this workspace manifold \(\ReachableWorkspace\), while an occupancy lookup in spatial memory confirms that the target volume does not intersect persistent static obstacles.
  2. Dynamic Reachability Transit Check: Even when a target lies inside the static workspace manifold \(\ReachableWorkspace\), it may be physically unreachable within the finite window during which its sensor evidence remains valid. This check evaluates whether the end effector can transition from its measured state \((\CartesianPosition_0, \Velocity_0)\) to the target pose within the remaining window \(\IntentLeaseExpiry - \CurrentMonotonicTime\) while respecting its velocity, acceleration, jerk, and thermal torque limits.
Craig, John J. 2005. Introduction to Robotics: Mechanics and Control. 3rd ed. Pearson Prentice Hall.
Lynch, Kevin M, and Frank C Park. 2017. Modern Robotics: Mechanics, Planning, and Control. Cambridge University Press.

10 Workspace vs. Singularity: Membership in \(\ReachableWorkspace\) says that some joint configuration reaches the pose, not that the arm can move freely there; inside the workspace the Jacobian can still lose rank at a singular configuration, where some Cartesian velocities become unattainable. A workspace screen therefore rejects unreachable targets early, leaving singularities to the configuration and path checks; Forward kinematics and singularities develops the Jacobian rank loss behind them.

Whatever continuous trajectory profile the actuators follow, no physical move covers distance \(\TargetTravelDistance = \|\TargetPosition - \CartesianPosition_0\|\) faster than its maximum speed ceiling allows. Here both \(\CartesianPosition\) terms are translation vectors in the same Cartesian frame, not full \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) poses; orientation feasibility requires a separate check. For terminal completion before the current expiry, a necessary condition is: \[\frac{\TargetTravelDistance}{\SpeedBound} \le \IntentLeaseExpiry - \CurrentMonotonicTime. \tag{6}\] Actuator acceleration limits, jerk ceilings, and motor torque-speed curves only lengthen the required transit time beyond this kinematic floor. A proposal that violates this inequality cannot promise terminal completion in that window. A request for an intermediate guarded segment may remain feasible if its executed motion and stopping fallback fit the current authority, deferring continuation to newly admitted evidence. Dynamic Reachability, Intent Envelopes, and Differential Games gives triangular and trapezoidal transit bounds and explains how jerk limits change the complete-move check.

The scalar lower bound is an early rejection test, not a trajectory generator. It avoids optimizing a terminal request that is already too late, leaving detailed acceleration profiles, obstacle avoidance, and stopping reserves to the fuller motion check. Equation 6 couples spatial distance to evidence lifetime, so dynamic reachability evaluates the target and its supporting evidence together rather than the spatial coordinate alone.

↪ Resolving the admitted goal region into a timed trajectory is developed in Replacement Timing and the Planning Horizon.

Definition 10.2: Dynamic reachability

Dynamic reachability is the property that the end effector can move from its measured state to a target pose, within its velocity, acceleration, jerk, and thermal limits, before the evidence supporting that target expires.

  1. Significance: It prices a target in time as well as space. At the arm’s 1 m/s TCP limit, equation 6 allows at most 219 mm of travel inside the mug’s 219 ms tracked evidence window, so a handle 300 mm away is dynamically unreachable on one capture even though it lies well inside the workspace.
  2. Distinction: Static reachability evaluates geometric feasibility alone (whether any joint configuration can achieve the pose). Dynamic reachability introduces the clock and actuator dynamics. Unlike the dynamic obstacle forecasting of section 10.6.1, which bounds where external actors might move, dynamic reachability bounds where the robot’s end effector can safely travel within a bounded lease.
  3. Common pitfall: Attempting to resolve an unreachable target by stretching its evidence deadline in software. Only fresh sensor measurements can renew an expiring window; the remedy is a staged approach whose segments each fit their own evidence and fallback.

On the arm, the mug’s evidence under the qualified tracker stays valid for 219 ms after its capture. At the 1 m/s TCP speed limit, the gripper could cover at most 219 mm even if the entire window remained. A terminal proposal issued with the gripper 300 mm from the handle needs at least 300 ms of transit. It therefore receives ERR_DYNAMIC_TIME_INSUFFICIENT if it promises completion on that record. Without the tracker, the full window is only 60 ms and the corresponding speed-only reach is 60 mm. Processing and transport consume part of either window. Acceleration further reduces the reachable distance.

This bound and the fuller checks behind it assume that actuators stay within their nominal thermal ratings, that encoders report the initial state \((\CartesianPosition_0, \Velocity_0)\) correctly, and that the payload matches the inertial model. When winding temperatures cross the thresholds of Thermal capacity and insulation breakdown thresholds, or a force measurement reveals a payload mismatch, the permission path lowers \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) and \(\SpeedBound\) (thermal derating). It then evaluates new proposals against the reduced limits and rechecks any admitted path that the derating may have made infeasible.

A rejection must not simply discard the packet, which would leave the reasoning model waiting for a task timeout. The intent filter returns a structured diagnostic across the proposal boundary: the classification (ERR_WORKSPACE_OUT_OF_BOUNDS, ERR_DYNAMIC_TIME_INSUFFICIENT, or ERR_OCCUPANCY_BLOCKED) and the numerical margin of failure. A dynamic failure reports the shortfall between the transit bound and the remaining window; a workspace failure reports the nearest reachable point on the boundary of \(\ReachableWorkspace\). The reasoning layer can then seek fresher evidence, choose another approach, slow the process, or propose moving the base. It cannot extend the evidence horizon without new sensor data.

The diagnostic makes a staged approach possible. Consider a setup where the pre-contact corridor and its stopping reserve are qualified, a closer wrist observation supports the 3 mm error bound, and the tracker holds the declared slip bound. The rejected terminal promise becomes a pre-contact observation goal. A separately checked corridor carries the gripper toward a better view while keeping a stop outside contact, and the closer capture supplies the mug-handle belief record whose error fits the 15 mm tolerance.

Better sensing does not shorten the physical transit floor. The 300 mm approach still takes at least 300 ms, longer than the 219 ms horizon of any one capture, so it needs at least 2 fresh captures. The planner executes guarded segments under chunk leases, and each continuation is checked against the current intent, target evidence, corridor, and fallback. The predicted route beyond the admitted segment carries no authority. Once the last segment and the closure pass the full trajectory check, the gripper completes the grasp inside the tolerance and moves to the admitted hold for handover. If the closer observation fails, identity becomes unresolved, or a renewal arrives late, the next segment is refused, and existing motion continues only while its own evidence, authority, and fallback remain valid.

↪ The staged approach, with each trajectory segment re-anchored to a fresh wrist-camera capture, is developed in Tail latency budgeting across a mission.

The 300 ms reach outlasts one 219 ms evidence window; it needs 2 captures.

10.6.1 Dynamic obstacle forecasting and forward occupancy

Even when the mug handle lies within kinematic and dynamic reach, a coworker may be about to cross the approach corridor, a conflict that the static workspace and transit tests alone cannot reject. Dynamic obstacle forecasting carries the perception and memory estimates forward into regions that moving obstacles could occupy so the intent stage can reject blocked corridors early, before the planner and permission path check the complete timed trajectory. Because this forecast predicts only where people might move and says nothing about whether those people can tell where the machine will move, it covers only one half of how coworkers and robots stay clear of one another.

↪ Motion and signals that let the people around a machine infer its goal early enough to yield are developed in Legible intent.

The intent filter consumes the dynamic track manifest \([(\text{ID}_k, \EstimatedPosition_k, \EstimatedVelocity_k, \EstimationErrorCovariance_k, \LastMeasurementTime, \text{state})]\) published by the multi-object tracker, alongside tentative detections, raw occupied regions, and unknown-space evidence, because failure to confirm an identity does not make its occupied region clear. For each confirmed or coasting track \(\ManifestTrackIndex\), the admission engine constructs a forward occupancy set \(\ForwardOccupancySet_k(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant)\), the region the actor could occupy \(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant\) after its last measurement: \[\ForwardOccupancySet_k(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) = \left\{ \CartesianPosition \in \mathbb{R}^3 \;\middle|\; \|\CartesianPosition - \EstimatedPosition_k\| \le \ActorBodyRadius_k + \HumanWalkingSpeedBound \FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant + \OccupancyCoverageMultiplier \TrackPositionDeviation(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) \right\} \tag{7}\] Here \(\ActorBodyRadius_k\) is a declared body envelope that covers torso, limbs, and carried objects. The term \(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound\) is a validated bound on the actor’s speed. The term \(\OccupancyCoverageMultiplier\) is a selected multiplier whose joint spatial coverage must be calibrated rather than read off a scalar Gaussian rule. The term \(\TrackPositionDeviation(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant)\) is the root-mean-square position deviation of the propagated track covariance. That deviation need not grow monotonically during coasting, so the check needs a conservative bound over the whole interval. \(\ForwardOccupancySet_k(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant)\) is called an occupancy set rather than a reachability set because dynamic reachability bounds where the robot can get in time, whereas forward occupancy bounds where a person might be. A tracker with a validated acceleration bound can instead center the set on the predicted position \(\EstimatedPosition_k + \EstimatedVelocity_k \FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant\) and grow it with \(\frac{1}{2} \ActorAccelerationBound \FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant^2\), which is tighter over short intervals. The speed-bound form is the conservative one that safety standards use.

↩︎ The dynamic track manifest and the tentative, confirmed, and coasting track lifecycle are established in Multi-object tracking and data association.

International Organization for Standardization. 2025. ISO 10218-2:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Industrial Robot Applications and Robot Cells. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2016. ISO/TS 15066:2016: Robots and Robotic Devices, Collaborative Robots. International Organization for Standardization.
Marvel, Jeremy A., and Rick Norcross. 2017. “Implementing Speed and Separation Monitoring in Collaborative Robot Workcells.” Robotics and Computer-Integrated Manufacturing 44: 144–55. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rcim.2016.08.001.

11 Approach-Speed Constant: The standard’s speed describes a design person walking toward a safeguard, not a measured coworker, and the standard applies a higher constant to hand and arm reach at short separations (International Organization for Standardization 2024). A forecast built on the walking value is valid only for the motion it describes. A coworker who runs or reaches across the corridor lies outside that bound, and the growth term must therefore be validated for the site rather than copied from the standard.

International Organization for Standardization. 2024. ISO 13855:2024: Safety of Machinery, Positioning of Safeguards with Respect to the Approach of the Human Body. International Organization for Standardization.

Equation 7 is the human half of speed and separation monitoring (SSM), a certified safety function that runs beneath the permission path and keeps a protective separation distance between the moving robot and a person. Certified safety functions beneath the permission path sizes that distance under ISO 10218-2:2025, which incorporates the collaborative-application requirements formerly published as ISO/TS 15066 (the technical specification itself remains published and is under revision) (International Organization for Standardization 2025, 2016; Marvel and Norcross 2017). The separation calculation assumes the ISO 13855 walking approach speed11 of 1.6 m/s for an approaching person (International Organization for Standardization 2024). The intent stage uses the same speed bound for an earlier and coarser purpose, rejecting a corridor before the planner spends time on it.

↩︎ The mapping of the robot’s protective-separation terms onto its stopping-distance budget is established in Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay.

Screening a corridor means sweeping the robot’s candidate transit volume \(\RobotTransitVolume(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant)\) forward in time beside every person’s set. On the aisle of figure 7, the coworker’s set grows at the ISO 13855 walking speed from 96 mm over the untracked horizon to 351 mm over the tracked one, and the corridor enters the larger disc, so the screen refuses it. Under declared motion bounds, the early screen requires the candidate transit volume to remain disjoint from every active occupancy set across the execution window. Here \({\ObservationAge}_k\) is the upper age of actor \(\ManifestTrackIndex\)’s last measurement at the current admission epoch, including bounded clock-conversion error, and \(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant\) is additional future time: \[\RobotTransitVolume(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) \cap \ForwardOccupancySet_k({\ObservationAge}_k + \FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) = \emptyset \quad \forall \FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant \in [0, \IntentLeaseExpiry - \CurrentMonotonicTime], \;\; \forall \ManifestTrackIndex \tag{8}\] Full trajectory admission must extend the exclusion check through the response delay and the complete stopping fallback. If a candidate corridor violates equation 8 by intersecting an expanding set, the early screen can return ERR_DYNAMIC_OCCUPANCY_CONFLICT and request a different corridor or a yield. The refusal diagnostic returns the earliest predicted conflict time \(\FiniteChange \EarliestConflictTime\) and the conflicting actor identifier \(\text{ID}_k\). Passing this screen does not establish a collision-free trajectory. The planner must supply the timed motion, and the permission path must check its swept volume and stopping reserve against evidence that remains valid. The arithmetic of this linear growth is simple; what decides the outcome is the horizon over which it runs.

Napkin Math 10.3: The freezing radius
Problem: A coworker walks in the 1.30 m aisle while the arm screens its corridor. How far can the growth term of equation 7 carry that person’s set over the mug’s evidence horizons and over a 2 s planning horizon, and beyond what horizon does one person’s set alone span the aisle?

Variables:

  • Approach speed \(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound =\) 1.6 m/s (Datasheet, ISO 13855 design value)
  • Evidence horizons 60 ms untracked and 219 ms tracked (Derived, equation 5); planning horizon 2 s (Illustrative)
  • Aisle width \(\AisleWidth =\) 1.30 m (Illustrative)

Math:

  1. Evidence horizons: 1.6 m/s \(\times\) 60 ms \(=\) 96 mm, and 1.6 m/s \(\times\) 219 ms \(=\) 351 mm.
  2. Planning horizon: 1.6 m/s \(\times\) 2 s \(=\) 3.2 m.
  3. Crossover: The growth term reaches the aisle width when \(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound\,\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant = \AisleWidth\), at \(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant =\) 1.30 m \(/\) 1.6 m/s \(=\) 0.81 s.

Result: Over the evidence horizons the set grows by centimeters to decimeters; past 0.81 s a single person’s set covers the full aisle before the body envelope and tracking terms are even added.

Systems insight: The duration of the screening horizon dictates whether the robot can plan feasible motion. Extending a dynamic forecast beyond the admitted motion and its complete stopping fallback can reject a corridor for possible human movements outside the current commitment. In narrow operating aisles, unnecessarily long forecast horizons can block otherwise admissible progress. The shorter screen must still cover response delay and deceleration; evidence expiry does not end physical motion.

A set that outgrows the aisle is the root of the freezing robot problem. Trautman and Krause showed that when a planner predicts each person independently and lets that uncertainty grow, the predicted occupancy of a crowd covers every path, and the robot stops even though people would make room for it (Trautman and Krause 2010). Three choices reduce unnecessary conservatism, and none permits shrinking a physically justified bound merely to obtain motion:

  1. Short receding horizons: Screening over the evidence horizon rather than seconds ahead keeps the growth term near 351 mm instead of meters. The forecast must still cover the entire executed prefix and the physical fallback, including response delay and deceleration; shortening the lease does not shorten an actor’s possible motion while the robot stops.
  2. Re-centering on fresh evidence: A qualified tracker re-centers each set on new measurements (\(\MonotonicClockInstant_0 \leftarrow \FreshEvidenceTime\)), resetting the growth term and the propagated covariance, provided its accuracy and latency support the required bound.
  3. Interaction-aware prediction: Trautman and Krause’s own remedy predicts the people and the robot jointly, so that each accounts for how the others will respond. Such predictions are learned or modeled proposals, and they may narrow a set only after their error has been bounded by the same kind of evidence the permission path demands of any other input.

The discs are centered on the coworker’s last measured position, so the tracker’s lifecycle sets their age. A confirmed track receives fresh measurements, while a coasting track accumulates propagated uncertainty and widens the exclusion region as its evidence ages. A goal proposal cannot refresh a stale track without incoming sensor data, and a detour proposed after a refusal needs its own timed check. When a person walks out of view, terminating the software track does not make the occluded space free. Memory already erodes certified free space behind an occlusion at the same walking-speed bound (Forward State Propagation and Occlusion Decay), and that unknown-space policy remains in force until new observations justify a different claim.

Checkpoint 10.1: Evidence windows and reach

A target is admissible only while its evidence lasts, and every early screen in this section is sized by that window.

Reachability and corridor filters reject provably impossible requests before planning begins. The survivors remain candidates, carrying the target bounds and obstacle evidence needed for the full motion check. The remaining challenge is distinguishing between failures these physical checks can expose and errors in task meaning that geometry and kinematics alone cannot resolve.

10.7 Intent Grounding Failure Modes and Their Checks

Whether an error arises from physical drift or from task misunderstanding, a vision-language model or multimodal transformer emits the same \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) target pose for a physical manipulator. A specular reflection in empty air, a stale coordinate from a moving belt, or a bolted fixture mistaken for a handle all arrive across the proposal boundary as the same clean seven-number pose:

  1. Phantom target (hallucination): Attention concentrates on a specular glare, a shadow, or a transparent surface, and the pipeline predicts an object where none exists. Missing or corrupted depth returns then place the target in empty space or inside a transparent barrier, and an admitted proposal sends the arm into air or into glass.
  2. Stale target (pipeline latency or temporal drift): The physical workpiece shifts on a conveyor, vibrates on a feeder tray, or is displaced by a human coworker while the multi-stage perception pipeline buffers, batches, and decodes frames. The machine’s intent model takes 336 ms per inference, longer than the 60 ms untracked horizon before any tail latency is added. A chunked action stream can keep the arm moving throughout, but on the target the model last saw. The resulting proposal arrives downstream with mathematically valid coordinates that describe where the object used to be rather than where it is, consuming physical clearance before motion begins.
  3. Semantic ambiguity (bimodal posterior or argmax failure): Two visually identical candidate objects (e.g., two red mugs) reside within the pick zone, and both score high because the detector scores each box independently. The calibrated prediction set holds both (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 2\)). Keeping only the top box or averaging the candidates’ centroids commands an arbitrary choice or drives the end effector toward the empty midpoint between the objects, risking a dual-object collision.
  4. Misclassified affordance: A bolted fixture is taken for a movable handle, or a thin-walled container for a robust one, and the requested grip force destroys it. The approach can be collision-free while the contact wrench is wrong.
  5. Dynamic unreachability (kinematic transit shortfall): A requested terminal move is geometrically within the static workspace manifold \(\ReachableWorkspace\), but the transit time demanded by actuator velocity and acceleration limits exceeds the remaining sensor evidence window (\(\TerminalTransitTime > \IntentLeaseExpiry - \CurrentMonotonicTime\)). The terminal request must be rejected or replaced with guarded stages supported by fresh observations; extending its old evidence deadline cannot make it feasible.

All five failures arrive across the proposal boundary in the same format: a candidate position \(\CartesianPosition \in \mathbb{R}^3\) and orientation \(\DirectionalCosineMatrix \in \SpecialOrthogonalGroup\). While a pose in empty air leads to a futile reach and a pose inside a fixture leads to contact unless path and permission checks intervene, the wrong-object case can satisfy every geometric and kinematic check while targeting the wrong item. Because the independent permission path checks only what it can measure (freshness, coverage, geometry, current state, and actuator limits), it makes no claim to verify the task’s meaning. The Safety MCU enforces physical feasibility, not semantic truth. A goal can be collision-free, kinematically smooth, and torque-limited, yet wrong for the task. Semantic verification therefore belongs to upstream task monitoring and operator oversight, and the same boundary holds under adversarial attack, where an injected instruction or a printed patch in the camera view still yields a well-formed pose.

↪ What the permission path can and cannot catch when the proposer itself is attacked is developed in Physical sensor spoofing and proposer injection.

Sajjan, Shreeyak, Matthew Moore, Mike Pan, Ganesh Nagaraja, Johnny Lee, Andy Zeng, and Shuran Song. 2020. “ClearGrasp: 3D Shape Estimation of Transparent Objects for Manipulation.” Proceedings of the IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 3634–42. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA40945.2020.9197518.

A fresh depth observation can reject a proposed grasp when required object evidence is absent inside a covered, visible region, whereas missing returns in an occluded or unobserved region leave that space unknown. On this station, the singulation rule goes further by requiring independently qualified depth evidence of a single candidate in the pick window, even when the grounding model reports high confidence. Because the Safety MCU holds no scene representation with which to count objects, this check runs in a separate station component before any proposal is forwarded. The component routes any window with multiple candidates to a coworker for manual selection (a scoped station policy; another qualified operating mode could select a uniquely identified mug among several objects). Counting itself depends on validated sensor coverage and segmentation, so it shares a failure mode with phantom targets. Touching items can merge into a single cluster, and transparent objects such as the glass jar return little or no depth (Sajjan et al. 2020). The counter’s qualified domain must therefore exclude those cases or route them to a manual pick. Even when the count finds a single cluster, it still cannot verify that the lone object is a mug, and while the producer’s calibrated ambiguity filter may refuse earlier, it cannot replace the station’s independent check.

Table 5 groups these failures by the check that can expose them. Dynamic transit insufficiency can be rejected before motion, whereas a misclassified affordance may first become observable through contact residuals. The table separates admission checks from responses after motion or contact has begun. At the cage-door latch of notebook 10.1, the 2 ms contact-loop response already leaves 39 N at brake-command onset, and the peak comes later. Precontact speed and force limits, actuator response, compliance, and available clearance therefore determine whether the full contact trace stays within the validated contact limits.

Every row of the table therefore requires the proposal to carry its spatial bounds, the provenance of its evidence, and its expiry in a form the permission path can check without the model. A rejected proposal returns a diagnostic reason to the learned layer without revoking an earlier active record, which remains usable while its own state, evidence, lease, and fallback hold. An admitted proposal stays subject to independent permission checks as the plant moves, and the record the permission path grants from it is the intent lease.

10.8 The Intent Lease Specification and Contract

If a target region, an actuator effort ceiling, and an expiration deadline travel as separate messages across the inter-core bus, a dropped or delayed packet can leave the trajectory planner tracking a new pose under an old force limit or an expired evidence timestamp. For the red mug, upstream perception and belief filtering distill raw sensor telemetry into those three quantities: a target region bounded by empirical evidence, an effort ceiling on actuator output, and an explicit deadline after which tracking must cease. The intent lease binds them into a single, immutable record for downstream control. Its lineage anchors to the belief record through a SHA-256 digest, while its evidence timestamp maps that record’s sensor transduction epoch directly into the Safety MCU’s monotonic clock domain. This admitted target establishes a verified envelope for downstream planning, leaving trajectory generation and execution permission to subsequent safety stages.

This stage emits the intent lease, the eighth record in the book’s sixteen-record chain. Authority divides cleanly between the two processors. The high-performance Application Processor generates an untrusted intent proposal (table 2), while the independent, real-time Safety MCU validates and latches the admitted lease. By binding the admitted target to a verified goal set, an affordance frame, and a collection of active safety predicates, this latched lease strictly constrains the downstream trajectory planner before the hardware admits any joint motion.

To guarantee deterministic processing on the safety controller, the proposal payload uses a statically bounded schema. Fixed-size fields eliminate dynamic heap allocation and pointer dereferencing, enabling the validation pipeline to reject malformed records in bounded execution cycles. However, static memory layout alone does not guarantee real-time deadlines. Inter-core memory copies, cache maintenance, lock contention, and bus arbitration must all remain within measured worst-case execution time bounds. The inbound proposal remains an advisory request from untrusted software; the Safety MCU retains exclusive authority over parameter bounds, expiration deadlines, and fallback activation.

↪ Mailboxes, memory barriers, and copy costs on the inter-core seam are developed in Heterogeneous SoC Mailboxes, Memory Barriers, and Zero-Copy IPC.

The covariance ellipsoid derived for the mug handle in section 10.3.3 represents an upstream producer estimate. Rather than serializing a dense, oriented \(3\times3\) covariance matrix across the real-time wire, the protocol projects these bounds into three axis-aligned position-error envelopes \((\XPositionErrorEnvelope, \YPositionErrorEnvelope, \ZPositionErrorEnvelope)\) alongside three requested task tolerances \((\XTaskToleranceSemiAxis, \YTaskToleranceSemiAxis, \ZTaskToleranceSemiAxis)\) expressed in the robot base frame \(\ManipulatorBaseFrame\). The componentwise test \(0 \le \PositionErrorEnvelope_i \le \TaskToleranceSemiAxis_i\) is a necessary preliminary screen. Admission must also establish that the full conservative error set, together with reserved controller tracking error, fits inside the ellipsoidal task region defined in section 10.1. Passing each axis independently does not establish that containment. Three axis-aligned bounds define a rectangular box rather than an ellipsoid, so a corner where errors peak along all three axes simultaneously can protrude outside the curved task boundary even when each individual axis stays within its limit, requiring the Safety MCU to evaluate the ellipsoidal norm at the worst-case corner of that error box plus the reserved tracking margin. The upstream producer must mathematically justify its projection from raw state covariance to these conservative error envelopes, accounting for camera calibration drift, latency jitter, and declared tail-risk percentiles. Angular tolerances and contact-mode transition rules derive from an independently certified task profile, allowing the wire proposal to transmit only the nominal target orientation quaternion.

Temporal validation hinges on the belief record’s evidence epoch (the physical instant of sensor exposure, not the time processing completed), mapped into the Safety MCU’s local clock domain. The Safety MCU verifies the parent digest and evidence epoch against retained belief records in local memory, adding the bounded synchronization error to the calculated evidence age. Requested expiration times reference this same monotonic hardware clock. Here, the parent digest establishes data lineage rather than cryptographic authentication, enabling forensic analysis to trace an actuator command directly back to the originating camera frame. Because the safety controller gates proposals exclusively on physical invariant checks and kinematic feasibility, the wire format omits cryptographic signatures, prioritizing physical truth over sender identity.

↩︎ Converting a producer’s evidence epoch into the Safety MCU’s monotonic clock, with a bounded synchronization error, is established in Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts.

An intent lease progresses through five discrete lifecycle states on the Safety MCU: Pending, Active, Renewed, Expired, and Aborted. The controller transitions a proposal from Pending to Active only after verifying all geometric and dynamic ceilings and confirming that a collision-free stopping path remains executable from the current state. Transitioning to Renewed requires a strictly monotonic sequence counter, a newer parent belief record with an updated sensor capture epoch, and a fully passed admission re-check. A complex approach trajectory can span multiple consecutive lease renewals as long as fresh perception evidence continues to justify the motion. Dropping an isolated camera frame does not immediately abort tracking if the current coasting window remains valid. However, absent renewal, the hardware timer continues counting toward expiration. When an anomaly, force limit breach, or evidence timeout revokes tracking authority, the controller immediately engages a pre-calculated deceleration or hold trajectory to manage the kinetic energy of the moving manipulator safely.

Figure 8 draws the same machine. Its one subtle transition is the self-loop on a stale renewal. A packet with an old sequence number or an old capture is discarded without resetting the timer, so a host that keeps talking without new evidence still runs out of authority. Expiry and abort end in the same place, the validated fallback that the MCU selects for the measured state.

Definition 10.3: Expiring intent lease

Expiring intent lease (\(\IntentLease\)) is a time-bounded target authorization issued by the independent permission path that binds an admitted target pose and effort ceilings to a monotonic hardware deadline \(\IntentLeaseExpiry\): \[\IntentLeaseExpiry = \min(\MonotonicClockInstant_0 + \EvidenceHorizon, \; \LeaseAdmissionInstant + \IntentAdministrativeLease, \; \StoppingDeadline) \tag{9}\] upon whose expiration dependent target-tracking authority is automatically revoked without requiring an explicit cancellation packet. The lease establishes geometric targets and effort bounds for downstream trajectory generation, reserving path execution authority for independent trajectory-level validation.

  1. Significance: Revokes tracking authority autonomously on a hardware deadline whenever upstream deliberation stalls, engaging a validated fallback response without depending on cooperative shutdown signals from the host processor.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a standard network keepalive or software heartbeat ping, an intent lease physically couples the temporal validity of sensor evidence (\(\EvidenceHorizon\)) with real-time braking feasibility, capping lease duration so that a validated stopping maneuver always fits within the verified spatial clearance.
  3. Common pitfall: Artificially inflating lease durations to mask tail latency (p99) in vision models or neural planners. Generous timeouts create dangerous open-loop blind spots where the robot sweeps through physical space after environmental conditions have already evolved.

The three terms in the minimization enforce distinct safety bounds that prevent different classes of failure across the cyber-physical stack:

  • The physical target validity horizon (\(\MonotonicClockInstant_0 + \EvidenceHorizon\)) marks the instant on the monotonic clock when unobserved environmental motion or state drift can exhaust the target-error budget remaining within task tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\). This term dates strictly from physical sensor capture rather than packet arrival, where \(\EvidenceHorizon\) is the admitted target horizon after reserving controller tracking error, bounded above by the upstream belief record’s evidence-only horizon (equation 5). Once this horizon elapses without fresh perception updates, the robot would be tracking an ungrounded hallucination, regardless of whether host software runs without error.
  • The software administrative lease (\(\LeaseAdmissionInstant + \IntentAdministrativeLease\)) establishes an upper bound, measured from the admission tick \(\LeaseAdmissionInstant\), on how long the Application Processor may retain active tracking authority before the Safety MCU presumes host failure. Even in static environments where objects do not move (\(\EvidenceHorizon \to \infty\)), operating system scheduling jitter, garbage-collection stalls, memory bus starvation, or kernel panics can freeze the host pipeline. The administrative lease \(\IntentAdministrativeLease\) forces the system to decelerate smoothly rather than fly blind. This administrative horizon differs from the trajectory chunk lease \(\ChunkLease\) of Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget: while a chunk lease governs the execution duration of an individual trajectory polynomial, \(\IntentAdministrativeLease\) bounds the persistence of the macro-level target goal itself.
  • The physical stopping boundary (\(\StoppingDeadline\)) dictates the absolute latest revocation instant from which a worst-case braking profile can still arrest motion within remaining swept clearance. Given a verified swept-volume clearance \(\ClearDistance\) and a dynamic stopping distance \(\StoppingDistance = \SpeedAtDetection\,\PostRevocationDelay + \SpeedAtDetection^2 / (2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration)\), the deadline evaluates to \(\StoppingDeadline = \LeaseAdmissionInstant + (\ClearDistance - \StoppingDistance) / \SpeedAtDetection\). Here \(\PostRevocationDelay\) is the post-revocation delay, the tick, bus, and brake-onset terms that make up the 22 ms stop onset. It is the tail of the full pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) of Delay consumes stopping clearance, not a replacement for it. The other terms of \(\PrebrakeDelay\), observation age and the lease, are exactly what the deadline itself measures, so charging them again here would count them twice. This kinematic bound assumes positive velocity (\(\SpeedAtDetection > 0\)) and nominal braking deceleration. When the manipulator is stationary (\(\SpeedAtDetection = 0\)), a static hold assessment replaces the distance formulation. Any dynamic change in tool speed, detected clearance, or actuator thermal limits requires immediate recomputation.

By taking the minimum across all three terms on the Safety MCU’s local hardware timer, motion authority terminates at whichever safety margin exhausts first: sensor evidence staleness, host processor silence, or kinematic clearance depletion. Upon expiration, whatever the state of the host processor, a hardware timer interrupt revokes tracking authority and triggers the fallback response the MCU selected for the current payload, kinematics, and contact mode. The choice depends on physical context. A powered position hold requires continuous motor torque but stabilizes a suspended payload. A controlled path deceleration bleeds kinetic energy within the clearance boundaries. Safe torque disable may protect the windings of compliant backdrivable joints but can drop a suspended load or leave momentum uncontrolled. Any terminal mode requested in the original proposal remains purely advisory. For instance, an upstream policy requesting torque disable while suspending a heavy payload cannot override the powered hold mandated by the safety controller. If no fallback can guarantee safety from the current state, admission rejects the lease before motion begins. Neither a 1 kHz discrete tick nor a hardware interrupt can arrest physical mass instantaneously. Mechanical deceleration is governed by inertia and actuator torque limits, so the clearance it needs must be reserved before any motion is admitted.

The admission pipeline enforces the four handoff invariants of section 10.4 as a staged series of validation gates, ordered from lowest computational cost to highest so that execution time stays bounded:

  1. Evidence and sequence validation: Ingest the proposal record from the IPC ring buffer. Instantly drop packets with obsolete sequence numbers or mismatched schema versions. Compare the parent_belief_digest and sensor epoch against locally retained spatial memory records. Transform the evidence timestamp into the MCU monotonic clock domain, incorporate the worst-case clock synchronization uncertainty, and reject the request if the resulting evidence age already exceeds the evidence horizon \(\EvidenceHorizon\).
  2. Kinematic and tolerance bounds: Verify that all pose coordinates are finite numbers and that the orientation quaternion is strictly normalized (\(\lVert \UnitQuaternion \rVert = 1.0 \pm 10^{-4}\)). Confirm that error envelopes and requested task tolerances satisfy \(0 \le \PositionErrorEnvelope_i \le \TaskToleranceSemiAxis_i\) along all coordinate axes in \(\ManipulatorBaseFrame\). Then verify that the full conservative error set, together with reserved controller tracking error, fits inside the ellipsoidal task region. Validate requested wrench thresholds against static actuator ratings and workplace occupancy constraints.
  3. Dynamic feasibility and fallback clearance: Evaluate current joint velocities, payload inertia, and sensor-verified spatial clearance to confirm that a valid stopping trajectory exists. Reject any proposal where the sum of reaction delay travel and braking distance exceeds available swept clearance (\(\StoppingDistance > \ClearDistance\)). The MCU does not count candidates in the pick window, because it holds no scene representation. The station’s singulation check runs before a proposal is forwarded (section 10.7), and a window it has not cleared never reaches this stage. This stage admits target geometry and effort bounds rather than a path. The trajectory planner proposes the full continuous trajectory, which the permission path must separately admit.
  4. Authority latch: Commit the verified target pose, admitted wrench ceilings, validated expiration deadline (\(\IntentLeaseExpiry\)), and pre-selected fallback policy into protected MCU memory. Arm the hardware countdown timer for the admitted interval. Advisory values from the untrusted proposal cannot write to drive registers.
  5. Runtime watchdog monitoring: On every high-frequency control cycle and hardware interrupt, compare measured joint states, motor currents, and contact forces against the admitted lease bounds. If the hardware timer expires or force limits are breached, immediately revoke tracking authority and engage the latched fallback routine. A missed renewal does not revoke authority by itself. It leaves the timer running toward expiry.

Stage 1 checks temporal freshness, stage 2 checks spatial containment and the effort ceilings, stage 3 checks the certified fallback, and stages 4 and 5 latch what passed and keep checking it while the plant moves. Together, these five gates emit the admitted target record consumed by the trajectory planner (table 6), establishing the formal contract boundary between spatial perception and trajectory planning.

By linking parent_belief_digest directly to the upstream belief record and emitting its own intent_digest to the downstream trajectory record, the intent lease schema (table 6) creates an unbroken, auditable custody chain extending from camera photons to actuator torque commands. Downstream trajectory planners can neither expand the admitted geometric goal set \(\GoalSet\) (equation 1) nor prolong tracking authority beyond expiration_deadline_ns.

↪ The trajectory record that embeds the lease’s digest and answers to its bounds is developed in The Trajectory Record.

Filled in for the red mug, the admitted lease links directly to the mug-handle belief record by its digest and sensor capture epoch. The target pose designates the 6-DoF mug-handle affordance frame, where localization uncertainty is bounded by the belief record’s 3 mm initial error envelope, well within the 15 mm task tolerance. The evidence horizon marks the temporal threshold where unobserved conveyor belt motion causes error growth to intersect this tolerance: 60 ms under open-loop conveyor drift, extending to 219 ms when tracking filters provide qualified updates under residual slip. Kinematically, the lease constrains the manipulator to a 1 m/s tool-center-point speed ceiling. Because the facility catalog registers no fragile payload profile for this mug, the wrench ceiling is the default profile’s, and the controller enforces default joint impedance limits, relying on plant-level sorting rather than online compliance adaptation. A cataloged fragile payload would instead carry an item-specific ceiling, bounded below by the squeeze force that holds the payload through transit (Grasp mechanics: Friction cones and force closure) and above by its crush limit. Grasping and subsequent handover constitute the target objective, while the MCU retains exclusive authority over the fallback trajectory.

The lease expires at the earliest of the three terms of equation 9, and the mug record supplies a value for each. The scenario chooses an administrative lease of 100 ms, long enough that one dropped wrist-camera frame does not revoke authority, and a verified clearance of 300 mm ahead of the gripper. The stopping term needs a deceleration the arm can be relied on to deliver, which is not the controller’s command limit. The arm’s handbook bounds its worst-case stop at 552 ms with the full payload at maximum velocity, measured under the stopping test of EN ISO 10218-1 (Franka Emika GmbH 2021). The handbook states only “maximum velocity” and reports the stop in joint angles, so assuming that stop began at the 1.7 m/s Cartesian limit gives a derived credible deceleration of 3.1 m/s². At the 1 m/s ceiling, that deceleration and the 22 ms stop onset give a stopping distance of 184 mm, so the stopping deadline falls 116 ms after admission. With all three countdowns started together, as in figure 5, the 60 ms evidence horizon binds first when the mug is untracked. A tracker stretches the evidence horizon to 219 ms, and the 100 ms administrative lease then binds before the 116 ms stopping deadline. Subtracting the controller tracking reserve, which a deployment must still do, can only shorten the admitted target horizon and bring expiry earlier.

Franka Emika GmbH. 2021. Franka Emika Robot’s Instruction Handbook.

This explicit record structure establishes a concrete boundary for automated fault-injection suites. Systems engineers validate the safety controller by injecting unnormalized quaternions, stale capture timestamps, excessive wrench thresholds, and conflicting fallback requests into the IPC stream, verifying that the Safety MCU deterministically rejects invalid proposals across all operational states and timing margins.

↪ Negating each record’s guarantees, intent lifetime among them, at its handoff to derive injected faults is developed in Inverting design records and the five-element chain.

Checkpoint 10.2: Expiring intent and fallback authority

Verify your understanding of how a proposal is refused or admitted, and how the lease granted from it is sized and revoked:

10.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Every failure pattern cataloged here stems from trusting an upstream message to mean more than it physically certifies. Real-time safety interlocks and local execution checks must refuse a proposed target, or revoke tracking authority over an active one, whenever its underlying sensory evidence expires or its spatial justification dissolves. Downstream execution monitors must evaluate evidence that the upstream proposer cannot falsify, enforcing safety invariants even while incoming inference streams remain syntactically valid and numerically confident.

Fallacy: A renewal computed from the same stale frame is a valid renewal.

In distributed computing and cloud infrastructure, lease renewals often function as software heartbeats. A client process demonstrates liveness and retains exclusive access by transmitting an uncorrupted keepalive packet before a timeout expires. In physical AI, however, software liveness implies nothing about physical evidence validity. If an upstream planner loops cleanly while the physical scene changes, keepalives succeed while the robot drifts blind.

Consider an intent model that repeatedly processes its last wrist-camera frame of a red mug, dispatching renewal requests with incremented sequence numbers, fresh issue timestamps, and identical target poses. Across the network, every field that a keepalive inspects appears healthy and up to date. No new photons, however, have reached the camera sensor, while the physical mug continues moving down the conveyor at 0.20 m/s. Because a sound intent lease dates its authority from the parent belief record’s sensor capture epoch \(\MonotonicClockInstant_0\) rather than packet arrival, the Safety MCU observes that the evidence epoch has stalled. As a result, it expires the target 60 ms after the original camera capture. A naive controller that evaluated renewals based on packet arrival time would permit a spinning software loop to retain tracking authority over an obsolete coordinate indefinitely. Advancing software timestamps on stale sensory data creates an illusion of liveness while unobserved physical drift violates clearance.

Architect’s countermeasure: Anchor lease validity strictly to the sensor evidence epoch \(\MonotonicClockInstant_0\) of the parent belief record; reject renewals that fail to supply a qualified newer capture record, including its error bound and bounded clock conversion.

Pitfall: Allowing an unexpired temporal lease to continue commanding motion when target spatial bounds are violated.

In distributed locking protocols, a lease grants exclusive, unconditional authority over a resource for the entire duration of its temporal grant. In physical AI, however, an intent lease is a coupled spatio-temporal contract: \[\IntentLease = \left( \GoalSet, \; [\LeaseAdmissionInstant, \IntentLeaseExpiry], \; \ContactWrenchCeiling \right)\] Temporal freshness (\(\MonotonicClockInstant < \IntentLeaseExpiry\)) is a necessary condition for motion execution, but never a sufficient one.

Suppose a manipulator arm lowers toward the red mug under an admitted lease with a grasp tolerance of 15 mm. If an unexpected belt slip shifts the mug beyond that tolerance while time remains on the countdown, the lease is still temporally active, but the target has left the spatial goal region \(\GoalSet\) that justified the approach. Continuing until the countdown timer expires drives the arm toward an empty coordinate or provokes a collision. The runtime permission loop must continually compare the latest qualified target evidence and its age-dependent uncertainty bound against admitted limits. It revokes tracking authority the moment an update shows that the handle has left the goal set, \(\|\TargetPosition(\MonotonicClockInstant) - \AdmittedTargetPosition\|_2 > \PositionalTaskTolerance\), where \(\AdmittedTargetPosition\) is the admitted target. Under a qualified tracker that target follows the tracked affordance frame along the belt, so the test catches slip beyond the tracker’s prediction rather than the belt motion the tracker already cancels. Running the control loop faster cannot reveal an unobserved slip; sensing delay and bounded error growth must both be accounted for in the response budget. A temporal lease confers authority only within declared spatial bounds; temporal freshness never excuses spatial divergence.

Architect’s countermeasure: Embed runtime spatial containment checks (\(\PositionErrorBound(\FiniteChange \MonotonicClockInstant) \le \PositionalTaskTolerance\)) in high-rate control loops, revoking dependent authority when qualified evidence or its propagated bound no longer supports the tolerance mid-lease.

Fallacy: A confident grounding needs no refusal path.

In machine learning pipelines, a high detector score is often treated as definitive proof of identity. Engineers transitioning to physical robotics are frequently tempted to save latency with shortcut logic: if confidence > threshold: skip_ambiguity_checks(). That shortcut treats a model’s score as an audit of the physical scene, and it fails across three distinct systems dimensions:

  1. Scores are not calibrated probabilities: Neural networks trained with cross-entropy loss are often overconfident (Guo et al. 2017), and calibration fitted on one training distribution degrades under distribution shift (Softmax overconfidence and calibration error). An open-world detector can assign a high score to a transparent glass jar or a printed decal because it represents the closest feature match to the prompt.
  2. Per-box scores do not measure uniqueness: Open-vocabulary detectors score each bounding box independently against the text prompt without normalizing across candidate proposals (Minderer et al. 2022; S. Liu et al. 2023). If two identical red mugs sit in the pick window, both score high. Downstream logic that naively keeps only the top-scoring box turns two valid candidates into an arbitrary choice, concealing real spatial ambiguity from all subsequent planning layers.
  3. The consequence is physical: A score describes prompt alignment in representation space rather than confirming that the physical workspace contains exactly one manipulable entity. The two jar cases of section 10.3 mark the line. When the jar sits beside the mug, both enter the prediction set and refusal is mandatory however high either score is. When the jar stands in place of the mug, a confident singleton passes every check, which no score threshold can catch.
Guo, Chuan, Geoff Pleiss, Yu Sun, and Kilian Q. Weinberger. 2017. “On Calibration of Modern Neural Networks.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 70: 1321–30.
Minderer, Matthias, Alexey Gritsenko, Austin Stone, Maxim Neumann, Dirk Weissenborn, Alexey Dosovitskiy, et al. 2022. “Simple Open-Vocabulary Object Detection.” Computer Vision – ECCV 2022, Lecture notes in computer science, 728–55. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-20080-9_42.
Liu, Shilong, Zhaoyang Zeng, Tianhe Ren, Feng Li, Hao Zhang, Jianwei Yang, Chunyuan Li, et al. 2023. “Grounding DINO: Marrying DINO with Grounded Pre-Training for Open-Set Object Detection.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2303.05499.

Workstation safety requires an independent singulation check on every intent proposal, drawing on evidence that the vision-language model cannot manipulate. An independent candidate-counting stage operating directly on calibrated spatial point clouds or voxel occupancy grids evaluates the physical workspace. If the pick window contains more than one candidate cluster, the station refuses the proposal with ERR_GROUNDING_AMBIGUITY before it reaches the Safety MCU, regardless of model confidence. Any earlier active record remains governed by its own evidence epoch, lease duration, and fallback strategy. Transitioning to a safe hold or operator handoff requires an admitted, physically feasible trajectory. A detector score reflects the model’s match to the prompt, not real-world uniqueness; keeping only the top box can select the wrong physical object.

Architect’s countermeasure: Enforce the selected station’s singulation rule using qualified independent candidate evidence; treat ERR_GROUNDING_AMBIGUITY as a first-class diagnostic that can request a feasible hold or operator handoff without automatically revoking a still-valid active record.

Fallacy: Neural networks can resolve bimodal spatial ambiguity by averaging candidate target locations.

A continuous regressor trained with squared error converges toward the conditional mean of its targets (Mode averaging when demonstrations split). When demonstrations pick mug \(\text{A}\) half the time and mug \(\text{B}\) the other half, that mean settles on the midpoint \((\DemonstratedTargetA + \DemonstratedTargetB)/2\), which lies on the empty conveyor belt between two valid targets or directly on whatever obstacle separates them. Because the robot’s collision-free operational space is not convex, the linear average of two feasible goals need not be feasible, and the gripper closes on empty air or collides with the inner edges of both mugs.

Suppose the perception system observes two identical mugs a few centimeters apart and submits their spatial mean as a grasp target. The fused estimate can appear deceptively sharp. Both individual detections may pass association and innovation gates (Multi-object tracking and data association), while an averaging estimator reports an artificially low variance that evades covariance expansion gates. However, the independent pick-window geometric check from section 10.7 still detects the presence of two distinct candidates, prompting the station’s admission gate to reject the proposal regardless of what the fused estimate reports. That refusal does not revoke an active, unexpired lease; transitioning to a safe hold must itself remain physically feasible. Generative policies that explicitly represent multiple modes, such as diffusion policies, flow-matching models, or Gaussian mixture models (Mode averaging and the decoders that avoid it), avoid arithmetic averaging, but downstream execution contracts cannot rely on policy internals. Before crossing the intent boundary, the proposer must commit to a single goal set anchored to a single tracked physical entity. Averaging bimodal physical targets can replace two valid manipulation candidates with a location that satisfies neither task.

Architect’s countermeasure: Preserve explicit multi-hypothesis distributions at the reasoning boundary (via diffusion policies or Gaussian mixture models), and enforce discrete candidate selection backed by the independent pick-window ambiguity gate.

10.10 Summary

Grounded intent transforms an unverified proposal from a learned policy into a bounded, expiring physical target. In this architecture, a foundation model or vision-language-action (VLA) policy proposes a target pose in \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\), a spatial error envelope, and effort ceilings. The Safety MCU operates as an independent gatekeeper, evaluating the proposal against kinematic and physical limits before admitting it as an intent lease. This lease anchors directly to a parent belief record in spatial memory and remains valid only until the earliest of three deterministic deadlines: evidence decay (\(\EvidenceHorizon\), derived from the belief record after reserving controller tracking error), host silence (\(\IntentAdministrativeLease\), guarding against communication stalls), and stopping clearance (\(\StoppingDeadline\), reserving the space needed to brake to rest). A hardware countdown timer enforces this boundary, revoking trajectory tracking authority without waiting for host confirmation. Because deceleration takes time, the worst-case response latency must be budgeted directly into the stopping clearance. Effort ceilings enforce mechanical safety alongside temporal deadlines. For example, when closing a cage-door latch, contact force continues to rise throughout the response delay of the contact-control loop. Stopping criteria must therefore account for the force already accumulating when the brake command issues, rather than assuming an instantaneous tripwire stop.

Tracking a red mug on a moving conveyor illustrates the multi-rate timing constraints that physical AI systems must reconcile. Under open-loop motion, target evidence remains valid for only 60 ms before conveyor drift carries the object outside the grasp tolerance. Meanwhile, the deliberative intent model executes at 2 Hz, updating its inferences every 500 ms. Because this deliberative period exceeds the evidence lifespan, the high-level policy cannot maintain an accurate target on its own. A qualified real-time tracker resolves this discrepancy by assimilating high-rate sensor observations to extend the evidence horizon to 219 ms, and its fresh captures, not the model’s proposals, renew the lease on a goal set that follows the tracked handle. The deliberative model only reconfirms object identity at its slower cadence, so the arm need not hesitate, slow down, or abort between inferences. Guided by verified spatial corridors, continuous observation, and qualified tracking, the arm completes extended approach maneuvers across sequentially admitted segments, culminating in a grasp aligned with real-time state estimates. When a lease expires, the controller executes a stopping trajectory pre-validated for the current kinematic state, safely dissipating the mechanical momentum already present in the moving links.

In safe embodied architectures, refusal is a first-class operational outcome rather than an exceptional failure. When an instruction points to an ambiguous referent, the candidate set fails the calibrated prediction threshold and the station’s singulation rule. When a proposed terminal motion cannot reach the target before evidence decays, the admission controller rejects the transit bound. Similarly, when a planned motion corridor intersects a coworker’s forward occupancy set, the early screening filter denies permission. Screening the admitted prefix and its complete stopping fallback, rather than a speculative multi-second route, can reduce unnecessary refusal in narrow, shared workspaces. The screen must still cover response delay and deceleration after evidence expiry. Each refusal generates a structured diagnostic record that informs the proposer why the lease was denied, enabling informed re-planning. Symbolic task plans generated by language models integrate through this exact mechanism, mapping symbolic operators into physical preconditions and testable motion predicates. Nevertheless, physical admission gates cannot see past the wrong-object premise, because a model that confidently grounds an action to the incorrect object can satisfy every coordinate, reachability, and contact-force check. Validating that semantic premise requires task-level perceptual evidence and supervisory human oversight, while table 7 collects the equations that carry the physical decisions, what each bounds or decides, and where each is derived.

Key Takeaways: Grounded intent at the proposal boundary
  • Leases grant bounded targets, not trajectories: The host proposes a target pose, evidence epoch, error envelope, task tolerances, and effort ceilings. The lease the Safety MCU latches binds them to a goal set and motion predicates that the trajectory planner may not expand, and it leaves the path to the planner.
  • Expiry runs on the permission side’s clock: A hardware countdown ends authority at the earliest of the evidence horizon, the administrative lease, and the stopping deadline, so a hung host loses its target without sending anything. A renewal counts only when it carries a newer capture; a repeated frame with a fresh sequence number renews nothing.
  • Target evidence decays with unobserved drift: At conveyor speed the mug’s evidence lasts 60 ms while the current intent inference takes 336 ms, so this model cannot renew the target without another source of fresh evidence. Only fresh observations or a qualified tracker, which stretches the horizon to 219 ms, extend validity.
  • Evidence deadlines differ from braking deadlines: Expiry is not rest. After the lease lapses the arm still needs 22 ms before braking begins and then its stopping distance, so the lease charges the stopping deadline as its own term rather than assuming the evidence horizon covers it.
  • Geometric feasibility does not guarantee semantic correctness: A learned model can ground an action to the wrong physical object with high confidence and still satisfy every coordinate, reachability, and contact-force check. Because physical admission gates cannot evaluate semantic validity, task-level verification requires independent task evidence and operator oversight.
  • Refusal is a primary operational outcome: Ambiguous object identity, expired evidence, dynamic reachability violations, and stopping distance constraints all trigger deterministic proposal rejections. Structured diagnostics let the proposer gather more evidence, choose a staged approach, or request operator assistance.

When a learned target misbehaves at the machine, the first question is which of its bounds was missing, not how accurate the model was. A target without an evidence epoch cannot be expired, one without an error envelope cannot be compared with a tolerance, and one without an effort ceiling cannot be stopped short of damage, so each omission turns a model error into motion. The remedy is rarely a faster or more confident proposer. On the conveyor, no tuning lets a 336 ms inference hold a 60 ms target. The fixes are a tracker that renews evidence, a slower belt, or a refusal.

The intent lease is the third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), written as a record for goals. The lease grants a target, never a path. It names where the gripper may end, how hard it may press, and until when, and it leaves the route to a planner whose motion must be admitted again on its own evidence. Authority a learned model obtains for a goal therefore never extends to the motion that reaches it, and it lapses on the permission side’s clock whether or not the model is still running.

10.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

How a word comes to name a thing was a question long before robots acted on the answer. Gibson (1979) gave the thing its modern name, the affordance, what an object offers an agent able to act on it, and Harnad (1990) posed the grounding problem as a question about symbols that point only to other symbols. Robotics made the affordance quantitative long before learned models proposed one. Nguyen (1988) reduced a stable two-finger grasp to a geometric test on friction cones, and Ferrari and Canny (1992) turned force closure into a quality measure in wrench space. Learned grasp generators now propose such grasps directly from point clouds (Sundermeyer et al. 2021; Fang et al. 2023), but their scores estimate success on a training distribution and are not the analytic measures they replaced, which is why this chapter rechecks every survivor.

Nguyen, Van-Duc. 1988. “Constructing Force-Closure Grasps.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 7 (3): 3–16. https://doi.org/10.1177/027836498800700301.
Ferrari, Carlo, and John Canny. 1992. “Planning Optimal Grasps.” Proceedings of the IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 2290–95. https://doi.org/10.1109/ROBOT.1992.219918.
Sundermeyer, Martin, Arsalan Mousavian, Rudolph Triebel, and Dieter Fox. 2021. “Contact-GraspNet: Efficient 6-DoF Grasp Generation in Cluttered Scenes.” Proceedings of the IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 13438–44. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA48506.2021.9561877.
Fang, Hao-Shu, Chenxi Wang, Hongjie Fang, Minghao Gou, Jirong Liu, Hengxu Yan, et al. 2023. “AnyGrasp: Robust and Efficient Grasp Perception in Spatial and Temporal Domains.” IEEE Transactions on Robotics 39 (5): 3929–45. https://doi.org/10.1109/TRO.2023.3281153.
Fikes, Richard E., and Nils J. Nilsson. 1971. “STRIPS: A New Approach to the Application of Theorem Proving to Problem Solving.” Artificial Intelligence 2 (3–4): 189–208. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(71)90010-5.
McDermott, Drew, Malik Ghallab, Adele Howe, Craig Knoblock, Ashwin Ram, Manuela Veloso, Daniel Weld, and David Wilkins. 1998. PDDL: The Planning Domain Definition Language. CVC TR-98-003/DCS TR-1165. Yale Center for Computational Vision; Control.
