Kaelbling, Leslie Pack, and Tomás Lozano-Pérez. 2011. “Hierarchical Task and Motion Planning in the Now.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 1470–77. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2011.5980391.
Ahn, Michael, Anthony Brohan, Yevgen Chebotar, et al. 2022. “Do as I Can, Not as I Say: Grounding Language in Robotic Affordances.” Proceedings of the 6th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 205: 287–318.
Liu, Bo, Yuqian Jiang, Xiaohan Zhang, Qiang Liu, Shiqi Zhang, Joydeep Biswas, and Peter Stone. 2023. “LLM+P: Empowering Large Language Models with Optimal Planning Proficiency.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2304.11477.

Symbolic planning began on a machine that moved. Fikes and Nilsson (1971) wrote the STRIPS operator language to plan for Shakey, a mobile robot whose actions did not always have the effects their operators listed (Nilsson 1984), and PDDL later standardized that language so that planners could be compared on common problems (McDermott et al. 1998). The geometric preconditions that STRIPS left to the robot returned as task and motion planning, which interleaves symbolic search with samplers that certify continuous facts (Kaelbling and Lozano-Pérez 2011; Garrett et al. 2021). Language models now write the symbolic half, by ranking skills (Ahn et al. 2022) or by translating a request into a planning problem (B. Liu et al. 2023), and neither step checks the geometry.

The lease comes from distributed systems. Gray and Cheriton (1989) bounded a client’s authority over a cached file by time, so that a crashed holder could not block the others, and chose the term by weighing renewal traffic against the delay a conflicting write must wait. The calibrated refusal comes from statistics. Vovk et al. (2005) built conformal prediction on exchangeability alone, and Ren et al. (2023) carried it to a robot deciding when to ask for help. The motion lease adds a physical term: it is sized by how fast the evidence behind its target decays, not by clock skew or message cost, and its expiry is enforced on a processor that the proposer cannot stall. For further reading, six works trace the ideas this chapter composes:

Vovk, Vladimir, Alex Gammerman, and Glenn Shafer. 2005. Algorithmic Learning in a Random World. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/b106715.
  • Harnad (1990) states the symbol grounding problem and argues that symbols acquire meaning only through their connection to sensorimotor categories, the premise behind carrying evidence with every proposal.
  • Garrett et al. (2021) surveys task and motion planning, from operator languages and samplers to the planners that couple them, and shows where symbolic plans need geometric certificates.
  • Gray and Cheriton (1989) introduces leases for file-cache consistency and analyzes how the lease term trades renewal traffic against availability, the analysis this chapter reworks with evidence drift as the term.
  • Angelopoulos and Bates (2023) is a practical introduction to conformal prediction, with the coverage argument and its exchangeability assumption stated plainly, as equation 3 uses them.
  • Ren et al. (2023) applies conformal prediction sets to a language-model planner, turning calibrated ambiguity into a request for help, the producer-side refusal of section 10.3.1.
  • Trautman and Krause (2010) names the freezing robot problem and shows why independent forecasts that grow with time block every path in a crowd, the trade-off behind the forward occupancy screen of section 10.6.1.
Garrett, Caelan Reed, Rohan Chitnis, Rachel Holladay, Beomjoon Kim, Tom Silver, Leslie Pack Kaelbling, and Tomás Lozano-Pérez. 2021. “Integrated Task and Motion Planning.” Annual Review of Control, Robotics, and Autonomous Systems 4: 265–93. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-control-091420-084139.
Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.
Angelopoulos, Anastasios N., and Stephen Bates. 2023. “Conformal Prediction: A Gentle Introduction.” Foundations and Trends in Machine Learning 16 (4): 494–591. https://doi.org/10.1561/2200000101.
Ren, Allen Z., Anushri Dixit, Alexandra Bodrova, Sumeet Singh, Stephen Tu, Noah Brown, Peng Xu, et al. 2023. “Robots That Ask for Help: Uncertainty Alignment for Large Language Model Planners.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 229: 661–82.
Trautman, Peter, and Andreas Krause. 2010. “Unfreezing the Robot: Navigation in Dense, Interacting Crowds.” Proceedings of the IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS), 797–803. https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS.2010.5654369.

What’s Next: From intent leases to trajectory planning
When the next inference pass is late, an intent lease fixes the admissible target region, the effort ceilings, and the evidence deadline, but it specifies no timed velocity or acceleration profile. It cannot tell an arm already moving toward the mug how to continue while the proposer stalls. Trajectory Planning begins from that moment. It turns the lease into a timed trajectory that joins the motion already under way without a step in acceleration, sizes its replacement deadline against the inference tail, and attaches a precomputed stop that runs even if no later pass returns. The trajectory record carries all three, bound to the lease’s digest, and the permission path admits the complete bundle before execution, because a valid goal does not prove that a moving arm can still come to rest.
